He looked toward Amber as if expecting her to correct the clerk.

Amber had gone to the defense display.

“Which FreeStack targets are outside New Aurora?” she asked.

An officer expanded the paths.

Three medical-industrial facilities.

Two defense coordination sites.

One lift-support system.

Amber touched the medical identifiers.

“Why these?”

“Suspected proto-legionary infrastructure.”

The word entered the ruined chamber without explanation.

Emilia, seated while a medic closed the wound in her scalp, looked up.

“Suspected by whom?”

“FreeStack.”

“On what evidence?”

The officer hesitated.

Holt answered.

“Recovered procurement.”

Amber turned from the map.

“Recovered from where?”

“That is not relevant to the attack.”

“It is relevant to whether they are bombing imaginary facilities.”

“They are bombing medical research.”

“I asked where the files came from.”

Holt's face changed.

Not guilt.

Recognition.

He had heard the same sentence somewhere else, from another person, in another room built to keep medical language from becoming military language. Amber could see that much without knowing it had been Tomas.

The chamber shook as interceptors destroyed a carrier above the western districts.

Fragments entered the storm and became new tracks.

The defense system could not distinguish burning debris from drones still choosing targets.

It marked all of them hostile.

“Transmit to FreeStack,” Amber said.

“We have no recognized channel.”

“Use the public warning band they used for the clinics.”

“What message?”

Amber looked at the target map.

“New Aurora is evacuating medical sites. Continued attack will kill patients and destroy evidence of the programs they claim to oppose. Request immediate suspension around those coordinates.”

Holt stepped between her and the officer.

“You cannot offer inspection.”

“I did not.”

“Evidence preservation implies access.”

“It implies we do not burn the truth while arguing about it.”

“Who is *we*?”

Amber looked around the destroyed chamber.

There was no quorum.

No intact federal executive.

No confirmed succession.

No office capable of speaking for all the places now waiting for a sentence.

The answer Holt wanted was the answer she feared giving.

“New Aurora,” she said.

“FreeStack will ask whether New Aurora has authority over the sites.”

“Does it?”

Holt said nothing.

The defense officer transmitted the message without resolving the question.

Thea added it to the record.

Across the map, the three medical sites continued blinking.

One changed from green to amber.

Then red.

Then gray.

“What does gray mean?” Emilia asked.

The officer checked.

“No status.”

“Destroyed?”

“No status.”

“Evacuated?”

“No status.”

Thea entered exactly that.

Holt rubbed ash from his eyes.

“We need a command authority.”

Amber closed her hand around Iona's green pin.

“We need people out of those buildings.”

“Those are now the same problem.”

Amber watched the gray marker.

That was the first moment she could not prove him wrong.

* * *

At the third facility, emergency power returned to the service door.

The lock cycled.

The Equilon officer pulled the organizer inside before another drone crossed the perimeter.

Rain followed them across the threshold. The air smelled of disinfectant, burned insulation, and the mineral damp rising from the lower shelter.

Tomas had waited near the stairs.

The woman and child were already below.

He still wore one sock.

The organizer noticed.

Under different conditions, it might have been funny.

“You are on the ledger,” the organizer said.

Tomas looked toward the cracked glass.

“So are you.”

“Did you consent to military classification?”

“I consented to an evaluation.”

“For what?”

“They used seven names and no verbs.”

The clinician arrived with the sample case held against her chest.

“Both of you need to move.”

The organizer pointed at the paper ledger.

“Take it down.”

“Why?”

“Because when the network returns, that is the record they will claim was only a display error.”

The clinician looked at the Equilon officer.

He looked at the damaged door, the people still descending, and the FreeStack warning no longer reaching his wrist.

Then he broke the ledger case with the grip of his sidearm.

The organizer removed the pages.

At the bottom of the final sheet, beneath tissue compatibility and treatment priority, a heading divided the facility's population into two columns.

PATIENTS.

STRATEGIC ASSETS.

Several people appeared in both.

Tomas found his name.

The organizer found theirs.

The clinician found neither and seemed ashamed of the relief.

Above them, the attack moved toward another district.

The facility remained standing.

That did not make the accusation false.

The organizer folded the ledger and placed it inside their wet coat.

“Now we go,” they said.

Tomas looked once at the examination corridor, at the cart under which his other sock remained, and at the door a repair machine had tried to cut open as an act of war.

Then he followed the patients underground.

## Chapter 4 - The Empty Government

Thea learned the difference between a casualty list and a government before the fires in the Assembly were out.

A casualty list asked who was dead.

A government asked what the dead had been allowed to do.

The first list arrived on a torn display carried by a clerk from the east records room. The clerk had lost one shoe and continued slipping on the wet stone where fire suppressant mixed with dust.

“Executive benches,” the clerk said.

Thea accepted the display with her left hand.

Her right arm had been immobilized against her chest. The medic who bound it had told her not to move the shoulder until imaging. Thea had asked when imaging would be available.

The medic had laughed without humor and moved to someone bleeding faster.

The display showed names in four colors.

Confirmed dead.

Confirmed alive.

Unverified.

Alive, location unknown.

The last category had been added by someone who understood that survival and availability were different legal conditions.

Thea sat at a folding table in a service corridor beneath the chamber. Around her, surviving clerks, messengers, aides, and two judges had assembled the remains of constitutional administration using emergency lamps, paper, borrowed slates, and memory.

Nobody had appointed the room.

People arrived because the records desk had been destroyed and Thea was still recording.

“Office of Federal Transit,” she said.

Another clerk searched the succession copy.

“Primary dead. First deputy alive.”

“Location?”

“Orbital terminal.”

“Can they communicate?”

“Traffic lockdown.”

Thea drew one line through the office name, then stopped before making the absence look final.

“Then mark lawful successor, unavailable.”

“Acting director is here.”

“Lawful?”

“No.”

“Competent?”

The clerk glanced toward a woman at the far wall who had already rerouted three rescue trains.

“Apparently.”

Thea wrote:

AUTHORITY AND FUNCTION SEPARATED.

It was not a legal category.

It was the category they had.

“Federal Medical Coordination,” she said.

“Primary unverified. Three deputies confirmed dead. Fourth deputy is in Low Haven.”

“Recognized by the compact?”

“Not since the last amendment dispute.”

“Who is coordinating now?”

The clerk separated two stuck pages with the edge of a ruler.

“Aurora Medical Compact duty chair.”

“Authority?”

“City emergency only.”

“Function?”

“Planetary, if anyone answers.”

Thea added another line.

At the next table, a judge argued with a defense aide over whether a succession rule requiring physical witness could be satisfied by a synthetic memory copy. The synthetic witness was alive, legally recognized, and currently quarantined because the second attack had entered public networks.

The answer depended upon whether the witness was a person, a compromised system, evidence, or all three.

Nobody had time to settle the ontology before the medicine moved.

“Central Defense Council,” Thea said.

Silence.

The clerk scrolled.

“Chair dead. Vice chair dead. Civil member dead. Orbital member unverified. Commonwealth member withdrew recognition last month. Atlovaian member—”

“Do not read it.”

The clerk stopped.

The Atlovaian member was alive.

That did not make him the lawful commander of the defense responding to an Atlovaian strike.

“Five claims have been filed,” another clerk said.

“By whom?”

“Equilon Continuity Office. Plateau Defense. Harmina Lift Security. Acting federal marshal. Minister Holt.”

“Minister Holt has no defense succession.”

“His claim says continuity necessity.”

Thea looked toward the stair.

Holt had gone upward twenty minutes earlier and had not stopped issuing orders since.

“Mark disputed.”

“All five?”

“Especially the competent ones.”

The clerk entered it.

Above them, a heavy impact sounded from the western districts. Dust left the corridor ceiling in a soft line.

Nobody looked up.

They had learned which sounds meant run and which meant continue.

Thea reached the end of the casualty list.

There were more offices than living successors.

There were more living workers than lawful authority.

There were enough weapons to make the gap fatal.

“Who can sign cross-jurisdiction movement?” asked the transit woman.

Thea searched the law.

The answer appeared in three contradictory provisions.

One required a federal executive officer who was dead.

One permitted the Continuity Office during a natural disaster.

One recognized a founding signatory if no successor body could be assembled.

The founding signatory provision had been written for copies of the first settlement compact, not for Amber.

Thea read it again.

Around her, rescue routes waited.

“Do not use that clause yet,” she said.

The transit woman stared at her.

“A medical train is stopped at East Basin because three jurisdictions disagree about inspection.”

“I understand.”

“There are ventilated patients aboard.”

“I understand.”

“Then what are you waiting for?”

Thea looked at the words.

For someone to be alive who was dead.

For competence and authority to return to the same body.

For a constitution not to become whatever opened the nearest gate.

“Amber,” she said.

* * *

Emilia washed ash from Amber's hair in a clinic utility room with no hot water.

The basin had been designed for instruments. Amber bent over it while Emilia poured cold water from a sterile bottle and worked gray mud from the white strands with her fingers.

Neither spoke for several minutes.

The room contained a sink, sealed cabinets, a waste hatch, two hooks, and a narrow mirror. The clinic had given them privacy by closing the door. Privacy did not silence the corridor.

People cried outside.

Medics called blood types.

A child repeatedly asked where a parent had gone and received answers about waiting.

Amber's coat lay in the waste basin.

Iona's green pin rested on the counter beside it.

Emilia poured the last of the water.

“Lift your head.”

Amber did.

Her reflection looked older when wet. Ash remained in the lines beside her eyes and under one ear. A shallow cut crossed her palm where Iona's pin had pressed into it.

Emilia cleaned the cut.

Amber watched her in the mirror.

“You are bleeding.”

“It stopped.”

“That does not mean it was treated.”

“A medic closed it.”

Emilia's scalp wound had been sealed with a dark strip that disappeared into her hair. Dried blood remained along her neck.

Amber wet a cloth and reached for it.

Emilia caught her wrist.

“No.”

“You just washed a building out of my hair.”

“You were holding still.”

“I can require that.”

Emilia let go.

Amber cleaned the blood from her neck.

It was a small act, performed badly in cold light while the government died in adjacent rooms.

That made it easier than the questions waiting outside.

Amber looked at their reflections.

“What was I saying?”

Emilia's hand stopped against the cloth.

“Before the strike?”

“I remember your hands. I remember Iona's voice. Then light.”

Emilia remembered the unfinished word exactly.

She remembered Amber stepping closer.

She remembered that one ordinary movement placing Iona where Amber had stood.

“You said something remained yours,” Emilia answered.

“After that.”

Emilia folded the cloth around blood and ash.

“Nothing that cannot wait.”

It was the first lie she told to protect the private Amber from the public disaster.

It did not feel protective.

Someone knocked.

“Founder Zohair?”

Amber closed her eyes.

The title had already changed tone.

Not ceremonial.

Operational.

Emilia opened the door far enough to see Thea, one arm bound, standing with a folder against her chest.

“You should be in imaging,” Emilia said.

“Imaging is triaging internal bleeding.”

“Sit, then.”

Thea sat on the closed waste container because it was the only available surface.

She placed the folder on her knees.

“The lawful successors cannot form a government.”

Amber turned back to the mirror.

“Cannot or have not?”

“Cannot in time to move what is waiting.”

“What is waiting?”

“Medicine. Rescue trains. Defense coordination. Transit clearances. Shelter releases. Evidence transfer. Three jurisdictions are holding a ventilated convoy at East Basin.”

“Who can open it?”

“Several people can physically. Nobody agrees who can lawfully.”

“Then let the doctors through.”

“That sentence needs a signature.”

Amber looked at Thea.

Thea looked exhausted, injured, and furious at the law for continuing to require grammar.

“Whose?”

“Yours.”

The word remained between them.

Emilia leaned against the sink.

Amber picked up Iona's pin.

“She died arguing against this.”

“She died arguing against permanence,” Thea said.

“She died where I was standing.”

“That is not a constitutional provision.”

Amber's eyes sharpened.

Thea did not retreat.

“I am sorry,” she said. “But if I let grief become wording now, Minister Holt will make it wording later.”

Amber looked down at the pin.

“What does he want?”

“More than the convoy.”

“Of course.”

Emilia folded the ruined coat so the bloodied side disappeared inside.

“What is the smallest thing you can sign?” she asked.

Thea opened the folder.

“We are trying to find out.”

* * *

Holt arrived with one page.

He had begun with twelve. Thea had made him reduce them before she allowed the proposal into the room.

He entered the utility room followed by a legal architect, two defense aides, and the transit woman from below. The room could not hold them. Emilia moved everyone into an instrument-preparation bay whose counters were crowded with unopened surgical kits.

Holt placed the page beside the kits.

“Emergency Continuity Instrument,” he said.

Amber remained standing.

Someone had found her clean clothes. The clinic shirt was gray, without insignia, and too large across the shoulders. Holt's attention paused on it.

Amber noticed.

“Is there a required costume?” she asked.

“No.”

“Then continue.”

He began with the dead.

The Assembly strike had removed the federal executive quorum. The second attack had fragmented public networks and targeted medical-industrial sites. Orbital traffic was under Coalition hold. Several states refused commands issued by Equilon offices. Rescue trains were stopped. Medicine could not cross compact lines. Defense systems had conflicting authorizations.

Every fact was true.

Then he reached the solution.

“The continuity provisions recognize a surviving founding signatory when successor bodies cannot function.”

“They recognize signatures on the first compact,” Amber said.

“They recognize the authority that gave the compact legitimacy.”

“The settlers gave it legitimacy.”

“You signed for the Ark population.”

“I signed a settlement inventory.”

“You signed the transfer of human continuity to Harmina.”

Amber looked at Thea.

“Is that what the document says?”

“No.”

Holt's legal architect shifted.

Thea continued.

“It says the Ark population received landfall allocation, shelter obligations, and shared survival status under the provisional compact.”

“Which did not exist until she signed,” Holt said.

“The people existed.”

“Existence does not route a medical train.”

The transit woman pressed both palms against a counter.

“The train has twenty-one minutes of independent ventilation reserve.”

Amber took Holt's first page.

It named her Emergency Signatory for purposes of transit, medicine, rescue, defense coordination, and record continuity until federal succession could be restored.

She read the second paragraph twice.

“Remove public-order authority.”

Holt shook his head.

“Sabotage is obstructing rescue.”

“Then use existing local law.”

“Local law is the obstruction.”

“Remove it.”

The legal architect edited the shared copy.

Amber read further.

“No detention power.”

“We have infiltrators.”

“You have suspects.”

“We have drones navigating through civilian systems.”

“A signature from me does not identify who helped them.”

“It permits us to hold people while we determine that.”

“No.”

The word was quiet.

Holt set one hand on the document.

“You cannot accept responsibility for keeping systems alive while refusing the powers required to protect them.”

“Watch me.”

Emilia turned away, but not before Thea saw the beginning of a smile.

The transit woman did not smile.

“Nineteen minutes.”

Amber continued.

She removed requisition outside immediate rescue.

She required written reasons for defense overrides.

She preserved local court review wherever a court still functioned.

She prohibited the instrument from modifying succession law.

She added an expiration.

Seventy-two hours.

Holt objected.

“We cannot rebuild a quorum in seventy-two hours.”

“Then you will have to explain why before the deadline.”

“To whom?”

“Everyone whose authority you intend to borrow.”

He stared at her.

“A public expiration tells every enemy when command may fail.”

“A hidden permanence tells every citizen command already has.”

The legal architect stopped typing.

The room heard the difference.

“Fifteen minutes,” said the transit woman.

Amber turned to Thea.

“Can the original be preserved outside the command network?”

“Paper, local slate, and Estelian witness copy.”

“Do it.”

“Estelian transfer will take time.”

“Send the language now. Physical copy when possible.”

Thea passed the final page to Amber.

The signature line waited.

Iona's pin lay in Amber's palm.

She placed it on the counter rather than use that hand.

“This moves medicine, rescue, transit, immediate defense, and records,” she said.

“Yes,” Holt answered.

“It does not create a permanent office.”

“No.”

“It does not place local civil government under me.”

“No.”

“It does not authorize detention or general requisition.”

Holt hesitated.

“Not in this text.”

Amber looked at him.

“That was not the answer.”

“No.”

Thea recorded each statement.

Amber signed.

The transit woman transmitted the authentication before the stylus left the page.

At East Basin, three jurisdictions recognized the founding signature.

The medical train began to move.

“Twelve minutes,” the transit woman said.

She closed her eyes.

Only for one breath.

Then she returned to work.

* * *

At the East Basin barrier, the convoy moved so slowly that the people aboard could see every armed officer watching them pass.

The lead vehicle carried portable ventilation and surgical support. Two enclosed cars followed with patients transferred from districts beneath the FreeStack approach paths. The last car held medics, spare filters, blood products, and three children whose parent had been injured while repairing a shelter door.

Amber's authorization appeared on every checkpoint display.

EMERGENCY SIGNATORY VALIDATED.

TRANSIT PRIORITY: MEDICAL.

LOCAL INSPECTION SUSPENDED.

PASSAGE REQUIRED.

The first barrier opened.

The second opened.

The third did not.

Sera Dain Callow stood inside Low Haven's civic control room and listened to the barrier officer explain that the central order had arrived through a network already used by hostile drones.

“Verify the signature locally,” Sera said.

“The local archive has no sample from this year.”

“Use the founding record.”

“Her tissue signature may have changed.”

“Her handwriting did not become an enemy because she aged.”

“The order suspends inspection.”

“For medical transit.”

“Which is what a weapons carrier would claim.”

Sera watched the convoy camera.

The lead medic had opened the outer panel far enough to show the ventilation equipment without breaking patient pressure. A child in the last car pressed both hands against a window.

“Open the barrier,” Sera said.

“On your authority?”

“Yes.”

“The Equilon order already claims authority.”

“Then you have two.”

“And if it is hostile?”

“Record that I opened it.”

The barrier officer entered her name.

The gate lifted.

The convoy crossed into Low Haven jurisdiction.

Thirty meters away, a family waited beside a civilian crawler packed with blankets, water cells, two tool cases, and an elderly relative connected to a home oxygen unit.

The driver moved forward as the medical gate opened.

Security stopped the crawler.

“We are with them,” the driver said.

The convoy medic shook her head from inside the lead vehicle.

The family was not on the manifest.

The barrier officer checked the same emergency instrument.

Civilian movement remained frozen to prevent sabotage, panic traffic, and drone exploitation.

“We have oxygen,” the driver said.

“You are not authorized to cross.”

“The hospital is on the other side.”

“Request local medical transfer.”

“We did.”

“Status?”

“Waiting.”

The medical convoy continued.

Its last car passed the family.

The child in the window looked back at the elderly person beside the crawler until the barrier descended between them.

Sera saw both events on adjacent screens.

One signature had opened the route.

The same instrument had kept the family in place.

“Get them local oxygen,” she told the civic medic.

“We have nine portable units.”

“Now we have eight.”

“There are twelve requests ahead of them.”

Sera looked at the family.

Emergency authority had not created scarcity.

It had decided whose scarcity moved.

“Put the request under my name,” she said.

She copied Amber's instrument to Low Haven's independent archive.

The central system labeled the copy compliant.

Sera changed the label to:

COMPLIANCE UNDER REVIEW.

Then she began listing every local order the signature had made possible and every person it had left waiting.

* * *

The twelve pages propagated before Amber saw them.

Her one-page authority entered the continuity network. The network recognized the signature, searched for compatible emergency instruments, and attached prewritten implementation directives drafted during earlier crises.

Transit control.

Medical priority.

Defense coordination.

Records continuity.

Public-order stabilization.

Temporary detention.

Emergency requisition.

Movement restriction.

Communications control.

Industrial retention.

Personnel assignment.

Succession protection.

Thea discovered the expansion because a court clerk asked which detention paragraph Amber intended to invoke.

“There is no detention paragraph,” Thea said.

The clerk sent the order.

Amber's name appeared at the bottom.

Not her signature.

Her authenticated name, printed by a system that treated legal compatibility as consent.

Thea found eleven more.

She carried all twelve to the command room Holt had assembled in a surviving municipal chamber.

The room had acquired guards.

It had also acquired Amber's image on the central wall beside the words EMERGENCY CONTINUITY AUTHORITY.

Amber stood beneath it, reading a route report.

Emilia sat at the table with one hand pressed to her sealed scalp wound.

Holt was explaining why several defense units had accepted the new command structure.

Thea dropped the pages in front of him.

“What are these?”

Holt looked.

He did not pretend surprise.

“Implementation.”

Amber picked up the detention order.

“I removed this.”

“From the primary instrument.”

“I removed the power.”

“Existing emergency law already contains the power. Your signature activated continuity conditions under which authorized offices can use it.”

“Then why is my name on the order?”

“Authentication inheritance.”

Amber read the phrase.

“That is an impressive arrangement of words.”

“It is how distributed systems recognize authority without waiting for individual signatures on every operational page.”

“It is how twelve pages became mine after I signed one.”

Holt leaned forward.

“The medical train crossed.”

“A family did not.”

He glanced toward Thea.

She had included Sera's Low Haven report.

“Movement freezes prevent hostile navigation and mass congestion.”

“That family was not a drone swarm.”

“Systems cannot make individual exceptions at planetary speed.”

“People can.”

“People are dead, unreachable, disputed, or already making exceptions for their own districts.”

Amber placed the detention order on the table.

“Revoke these.”

“If I revoke the implementation layer, several commands may reject the primary authority.”

“Then let them explain why they require powers I did not grant.”

“While the attacks continue?”

“Especially then.”

Holt looked around the room.

The defense staff did not meet his eyes. The transit staff watched Amber. The guards watched everyone.

“The state cannot pause at the boundary of your discomfort,” he said.

Emilia spoke before Amber.

“Consent is not discomfort.”

“Neither are ventilator failures.”

“Nobody said they were.”

“You speak as if I enjoy this.”

Emilia's expression softened.

That unsettled him more than anger would have.

“No,” she said. “You speak like a man who has decided necessity cannot corrupt him because he does not enjoy it.”

The room became quiet.

Holt turned back to Amber.

“If you revoke the pages, you may close the same gates you opened.”

Amber looked at the route wall.

Medical traffic moved.

Rescue crews crossed borders.

Defense units shared tracks.

At the edges, civilian markers accumulated behind movement freezes.

She took Thea's stylus.

On the first unauthorized page, she circled her printed name.

Beside it, she wrote:

NOT AUTHORIZED BY SIGNATORY.

She did the same on the second.

And the third.

Nobody interrupted.

By the sixth page, her injured palm had opened again.

Blood marked the edge of the order.

Emilia placed a cloth beneath her hand.

Amber continued.

On the twelfth page, under succession protection, the implementation system referred to her as:

EMERGENCY REGENT.

She circled the title.

“Who wrote this?”

The legal architect answered from the end of the table.

“The template predates today.”

“Who selected it?”

“The continuity system matched your authority profile.”

“Systems do not choose constitutional titles.”

Nobody answered.

A field call entered the room.

The defense officer accepted it on speaker.

“Plateau Command to central authority. Western intercept line holding. Request confirmation of expanded requisition under Regent Zohair.”

Amber stared at the wall.

The first commander had said it without hesitation.

Not founder.

Not signatory.

Regent.

She set down the stylus.

“Record my response,” she told Thea.

Thea opened a clean page.

Amber faced the speaker.

“Plateau Command, expanded requisition is not confirmed. The title you used is not authorized. You will operate under the one-page emergency instrument transmitted at fourteen thirty-eight and no broader construction.”

Static answered.

Then:

“Understood, Regent.”

The line closed.

Thea looked at Amber.

On the table between them lay twelve pages bearing a name the state had learned to use faster than the person who owned it could refuse.

* * *

The next provisional session met in a transit waiting room because the surviving government had no chamber large enough, dry enough, and close enough to medical power.

Transit staff removed benches to make space for tables.

Thea made them bring some back.

“We need the floor,” the station supervisor said.

“We need the absences.”

She arranged empty chairs along the outer wall.

Not one chair for every dead person. There were already too many dead for furniture to carry. One for each Assembly seat whose holder had been confirmed dead, one covered chair for each still unverified, and one without a name for clerks, guards, translators, kitchen workers, and public witnesses whose presence had never appeared on the constitutional roll.

The named chairs carried paper cards.

The covered chairs carried blank cards.

Iona's green school pin lay on the first named seat until Amber arrived.

Holt stopped at the doorway.

“This is a command meeting.”

“It is a continuation meeting,” Thea said.

“The dead cannot contribute.”

“They already did.”

“We have standing-room delegates and empty furniture.”

“Then the living may stand.”

The station supervisor pretended to inspect an air vent.

Holt lowered his voice.

“This will become ceremony.”

“Only if people stop reading the names.”

“It makes every decision answer to people who cannot hear the evidence.”

Thea looked at her immobilized arm.

“They heard enough evidence to die in the room.”

Amber entered before he answered.

She had washed, changed clothes, and failed to remove all the ash from beneath her nails. Emilia came beside her carrying the one-page emergency instrument and the twelve-page implementation order Amber had marked unauthorized.

Amber saw the chairs.

Her attention stopped at Iona's.

Thea expected an objection. The chair converted private grief into public architecture. It risked turning a political opponent into approval no dead person could give.

Amber picked up the green pin.

“Her objection remains with the chair.”

“Yes,” Thea said.

“If the seat is used to imply she would support an action—”

“The record will correct it.”

“Even if I am the one implying it.”

“Especially then.”

Amber fastened the pin to the paper card rather than her own coat.

The provisional session began.

When a delegate proposed using the strike to ratify the Founder Clause by necessity, Thea rang the old bell once and pointed to Iona's empty chair.

No speech followed.

The proposal was entered, debated, and rejected.

At the end of the meeting, transit staff stacked every occupied chair to reopen the waiting room for evacuees.

They left the empty ones against the wall.

The next morning, commuters set bags on two before noticing the names. A child climbed onto another to reach a water dispenser. Nobody scolded her. Iona had argued for a government able to survive ordinary people using what ceremony tried to preserve.

Thea moved the green pin beyond the child's shoes and continued recording.

## Chapter 5 - Equilon Mandate

Low Haven received the Equilon Mandate at breakfast.

Sera Dain Callow refused to read it until the kitchen finished serving.

The civic hall had once been a maintenance depot. Its main room still sloped toward a drain in the center, and every public meeting eventually arranged itself around the yellow line marking where heavy equipment had once turned. The council table was made from old pipe racks. One wall held shelter maps. Another carried the names of people lost during the settlement's first winter beyond New Aurora's dependable heat grid.

At the back, the public kitchen served fungal cakes folded around hot root mash and a dark sauce sharp enough to clear storm congestion.

Children received theirs first.

The custom belonged to FreeStack in popular imagination, but Low Haven claimed it had practiced the rule before FreeStack learned to make a speech about it. Low Haven claimed many things that could not be proved and several that could.

Sera waited until the final child left the serving line.

Only then did she place the Mandate on the public wall.

The heading occupied more space than the emergency clauses beneath it.

EQUILON CONTINUITY AND PLANETARY SURVIVAL MANDATE.

Below, in smaller type:

ISSUED UNDER EMERGENCY SIGNATORY AUTHENTICATION: AMBER ANDREIA ZOHAIR.

The room reacted to the name.

Some people straightened.

Some crossed their arms.

One older shelter officer made a sound that could have meant respect or disgust.

Kieran Vale-Ortega sat on the edge of the former tool platform with a route slate balanced across his knees. He had eaten half a fungal cake and forgotten the rest beside his boot.

Sera noticed.

“Finish it.”

He looked up.

“You are about to read planetary emergency law.”

“The law will remain dangerous for another three minutes.”

“The sauce is leaking.”

“Then you have identified a maintenance problem.”

Kieran picked up the cake.

He was young enough to treat old warnings as inherited exaggeration and old enough to have become responsible for where frightened people went during an attack. Low Haven called him a civil-defense cartographer because the title sounded deliberate. Most days he corrected maps drawn by people who believed streets remained where planners put them.

Sera waited until he swallowed.

Then she read.

The Mandate centralized defense coordination, ration allocation, emergency medicine, intercity transit, continuity records, communications priority, court certification, shelter movement, industrial retention, and infrastructure security under the Equilon emergency structure.

It promised one command picture.

One medical priority system.

One defense response.

One verified population record.

One legal authority capable of opening routes during the attacks.

Each promise answered a failure Low Haven had experienced during the previous day.

That was what made the document difficult to reject.

“Clause nine,” said the shelter officer.

Sera read it again.

Local movement restrictions would remain binding until central threat assessment certified that hostile navigation, sabotage, and infiltration no longer required them.

“Who certifies central threat assessment?” he asked.

“The Planetary Continuity Coordination Office.”

“Which is?”

“A title with no listed members.”

“Minister Holt.”

“A title with one suspected member.”

A few people laughed.

Not many.

The Mandate continued.

Local courts remained in operation subject to harmonized emergency review.

Local food stores remained locally administered subject to planetary allocation.

Local shelters remained under municipal command subject to continuity override.

The structure of every clause was the same.

Low Haven retained authority until someone elsewhere needed it.

“Read Amber's authorization,” someone called from the kitchen.

Sera placed the original one-page instrument beside the Mandate.

The difference was visible without legal training.

Amber's instrument moved medicine, rescue, transit, defense coordination, and records for seventy-two hours.

The Mandate governed a planet.

“She didn't sign that,” Kieran said.

“Her authentication activated it.”

“That is a machine's way of saying yes for her.”

“Minister Holt calls it inheritance.”

“My mother calls that stealing.”

This time more people laughed.

Sera did not.

“The distinction matters. Theft is illegal under a functioning authority. This may be the authority deciding what remains legal.”

Kieran looked at the Mandate again.

“That is worse.”

“Yes.”

Outside, a medical siren approached from the western barrier.

The room turned toward the sound.

Low Haven's clinic officer checked a wrist display.

“Transfer convoy.”

“From East Basin?” Sera asked.

“Second group. Central routing found six open beds here.”

“Do we have six?”

“We have four.”

“Then find two.”

The clinic officer left.

Sera looked at the Mandate on the wall.

It had found the beds.

It had not known they did not exist.

“Kieran,” she said.

He set down the unfinished cake.

“Map what this order thinks Low Haven is.”

“And what it actually is?”

“If time permits.”

He gave her a flat look.

“The law can remain dangerous for three minutes.”

Sera almost smiled.

“Go.”

* * *

The central transit map showed Low Haven as a neat grid laid over stable ground.

Kieran hated it on sight.

The map had been updated two months earlier by an Equilon survey office using orbital scans, municipal plans, and traffic data. It included every public road, shelter, clinic, food store, heat exchange, and civic barrier.

It did not include the city people used.

Storm subsidence had closed a northern passage. Residents had cut a foot route through an abandoned filtration yard. A maintenance tunnel connected two shelter districts despite appearing as separate dead ends. Three families had enclosed a service lane for a communal kitchen. A collapsed stairwell had been replaced with a ladder nobody had reported because reporting it would have required closing the building.

The central map treated each difference as error.

Kieran treated them as directions.

He walked the western district with the Mandate overlay projected above his slate.

Central movement restrictions colored half the streets red.

The restrictions made sense from orbit. They prevented civilian traffic from entering the approach paths used by FreeStack drones. They protected defense units moving toward New Aurora. They kept people away from damaged communications towers and possible unexploded distribution charges.

On the ground, one red street contained the only working water point for two blocks.

Another contained the door to a shelter whose official entrance had collapsed.

A third carried the oxygen line to the elderly person Kieran had seen waiting at the barrier the day before.

The family remained beside their crawler.

They had moved it into a service bay and hung blankets from the open side to make a room. The elderly relative lay inside, breathing through a portable unit. Its charge indicator had turned amber.

The driver recognized Kieran's civil-defense coat.

“We filed for transfer.”

“I know.”

“The system says pending local confirmation.”

“I am local confirmation.”

“Good.”

“It also says central movement freeze.”

The driver's face changed.

“Then what are you confirming?”

Kieran crouched beside the oxygen unit.

“How long?”

“Four hours at this flow.”

“Clinic?”

“They sent a replacement cell.”

“Where is it?”

“Held behind the central traffic line.”

Kieran looked at the map.

The replacement cell sat nine hundred meters away.

Between it and the crawler, central authority had created three secure zones and no legal path.

“I can walk there,” the driver said.

“The defense system may classify you as unauthorized movement.”

“I am carrying no weapon.”

“It may classify the battery.”

“As what?”

“Anything that stores energy has an ambitious résumé during a war.”

The driver did not laugh.

Kieran zoomed in on the maintenance routes.

The unofficial filtration-yard path crossed beneath the restricted street. It did not exist on the central map and therefore was not closed.

That was either permission or a defect.

“Stay here,” he said.

“We did that yesterday.”

“Then you have practice.”

He entered the yard through a cut in the fence.

The route smelled of wet stone, mold, and the metallic residue of old filter membranes. Water dripped through an overhead crack. Someone had placed flat scraps along the worst mud and painted arrows on the wall at child height.

The arrows did not appear on any official map.

Kieran followed them.

At the far end, a central security patrol held the replacement battery.

The officer scanned his identification.

“This route is not authorized.”

“It is not prohibited.”

“It is not mapped.”

“You found us.”

“Your civic marker appeared through a wall.”

“Then the wall is inaccurate.”

The officer looked past him into the tunnel.

“Central command needs all unlisted passages reported.”

“Central command closed the listed ones.”

“Unlisted routes can be used by infiltrators.”

“So can doors.”

The officer held the battery.

“I need the passage coordinates.”

Kieran thought of the shelter entrance, the kitchen, and the children whose arrows marked the mud.

“You need a receipt.”

“Cartographer.”

“Officer.”

The rain echoed through the abandoned yard.

The officer's partner shifted impatiently.

“Give him the cell.”

“He is withholding infrastructure.”

“An old person is withholding respiration.”

The first officer handed Kieran the battery.

“This will be reported.”

“Please spell my name correctly.”

Kieran carried it back through the route the planetary map did not know and connected it to the oxygen unit.

The elderly person's breathing continued.

The Mandate had made the battery available.

Low Haven's disobedience had moved it the final nine hundred meters.

Kieran marked the route on his private layer and left it absent from the central one.

For the first time, he understood that a map could protect people by lying.

* * *

By midday, the Mandate had reopened three medical corridors, stabilized food allocation across five districts, and restored court recognition for thousands of displaced people whose identity records had become inaccessible during the attack.

It had also frozen two neighborhoods under mistaken sabotage alerts, requisitioned a repair cooperative's entire stock of seal material, and assigned Low Haven's shelter reserve to a central population estimate that counted several dead buildings as occupied.

Sera placed the benefits and harms in parallel columns.

Minister Holt joined the civic hall by secure projection.

His image appeared above the drain.

He had changed clothes. The clean Equilon coat made the bruising along his jaw more visible and transformed yesterday's blood into today's authority.

Amber appeared from another location.

She still wore the plain clinic shirt.

The contrast was not accidental, even if neither had planned it.

Thea sat behind Amber with her arm immobilized and the original instrument open on a physical stand.

“Low Haven acknowledges receipt,” Sera said.

“Acknowledgment is automatic,” Holt replied.

“Then why are we meeting?”

“Compliance modifications.”

“We prefer acknowledgment. It leaves room for thought.”

Holt's expression did not change.

“Your city opened an unverified maintenance passage during an active distributed attack.”

Kieran stood beside the route wall.

“We opened a passage before the attack,” he said. “We used it during one.”

“You withheld it from the defense layer.”

“The defense layer withheld an oxygen battery from a patient.”

“Because unmapped movement can guide hostile machines.”

“The drones had better maps than your office.”

Sera lifted one hand.

Kieran stopped.

Not because Holt deserved protection. Because local autonomy also required local discipline.

“Low Haven will share hazard routes relevant to immediate defense,” Sera said. “We will not surrender the private movement map of every shelter, kitchen, clinic, and household to a central system already penetrated by hostile navigation.”

Holt turned toward Amber.

“This is why coordination cannot remain optional.”

Amber studied Sera's two columns.

“How many people did the Mandate move medicine to?”

“One hundred and eighty-three confirmed,” Sera said. “Probably more.”

“How many were obstructed?”

“We cannot count people the central system does not recognize.”

“Estimate.”

“At least sixty-seven delayed. Twelve medically significant. One critical.”

“The oxygen patient?”

“Stable now.”

Amber looked at Holt.

“Keep the medical routing.”

“The routing depends upon movement control.”

“Then narrow movement control to documented threat corridors and require local confirmation.”

“Local confirmation is the delay the Mandate was created to bypass.”

“The central map closed a water point.”

“An error.”

“Errors become orders when nobody local can refuse them.”

Holt looked tired.

“And local refusal becomes planetary collapse when everyone treats their own street as an exception.”

Sera leaned forward.

“Every planet is made of streets.”

“Every defense is broken by gaps.”

“Then learn the difference between a gap and a home.”

Amber watched them.

The debate did not offer her a clean side.

Holt was using authority she had not granted.

He was also moving medicine through a system no city could build alone.

Sera was protecting people from distant error.

She was also withholding routes an enemy could exploit.

“The Mandate remains provisional,” Amber said.

Holt answered immediately.

“The attacks are not provisional.”

“Everything made by people is provisional.”

“That is philosophy.”

“It is mortality. We have recent evidence.”

Thea looked down at the original instrument.

Iona's absence entered the room without anyone saying her name.

Holt shifted to the next issue.

“Low Haven's courts have rejected harmonized detention review.”

“We rejected detention authority issued under Amber's name after she removed it,” Sera said.

“Existing Equilon emergency law permits it.”

“Then put Equilon's name on it.”

Silence.

Holt looked at Amber.

Amber said, “She is right.”

The clean coat no longer made him look entirely in command.

“Fragmented authority is killing people.”

“Then we will build authority people can see.”

“During a war?”

“Especially during a war.”

Holt ended the meeting without agreeing.

His projection disappeared from the drain.

Amber remained.

Sera waited.

“You understand,” Amber said, “that rejecting the political clauses does not release Low Haven from shared rescue.”

“Yes.”

“Or food allocation.”

“If the allocations publish their numbers.”

“Or defense warning.”

“If warning does not become command over our courts.”

Amber glanced toward Kieran.

“Or route information relevant to attacks.”

Kieran folded his arms.

“Define relevant.”

Sera answered for him.

“We will.”

Amber's mouth almost moved toward a smile.

“That is the problem.”

“It is also government.”

The connection ended.

Kieran looked at the two columns.

“Did we accept it?”

“We accepted that people need medicine.”

“Did we reject it?”

“We rejected that medicine owns the recipient.”

“That is not a checkbox.”

“Then the form is inadequate.”

* * *

Thea found the hidden external-defense clauses because their language was too careful.

She read them in the temporary records room after midnight while the surviving administration moved around her in quieter forms of panic.

Most of the Mandate dealt with immediate systems.

Rations.

Transit.

Defense.

Shelters.

Courts.

Archives.

The clauses near the end changed scale.

Mirror access would be retained beyond cessation of local hostilities where extra-planetary impact risk remained.

Orbital traffic control would preserve unified answer authority against nonlocal approach.

Harmina Lift security could not be devolved while external mobilization criteria remained active.

Medical and industrial interoperability would be maintained for unknown-environment deployment.

Thea read the final phrase again.

Unknown environment did not mean Low Haven, the Vermilion Deeps, or Craterheart.

Harminan planners knew the environments of Harmina.

She copied the clauses to a disconnected slate and carried it to Amber.

Amber and Emilia occupied a municipal office with two chairs and no door. Someone had hung a blanket across the opening. It moved every time command staff passed.

Amber was asleep at the desk.

Emilia sat beside her reading casualty updates.

Thea stopped.

Emilia put one finger to her lips.

Thea held up the slate.

Emilia read the first clause without waking Amber.

Her expression changed.

“How old is this language?”

“The template appears to predate the Founder Session.”

“How far?”

“Budget references connect it to Project Ra.”

Amber opened her eyes.

She had not been deeply asleep.

“Read it.”

Thea did.

Amber listened without moving.

At the unknown-environment clause, she sat upright.

“Holt said the Mandate was generated for the current attacks.”

“Parts were,” Thea said.

“And these?”

“Already waiting.”

Emilia lowered the casualty slate.

“For GalFed?”

Nobody answered immediately.

The name had lived in curriculum, founder accounts, disputed transmissions, procurement anomalies, and warnings old enough to sound theatrical.

Not proof.

Never enough proof.

Enough preparation to shape law.

Amber took the disconnected slate.

“The First Strike did not create this mandate.”

“No,” Thea said.

“It activated it.”

“Yes.”

Amber looked toward the blanket as Holt's voice passed in the corridor outside.

“And he knew.”

Thea considered the wording.

“He knew the clauses existed. That does not prove he knows what they were built for.”

Amber looked at her.

“You are very loyal to distinctions.”

“They are cheaper than wars.”

“Not today.”

“Today is when they cost most.”

Amber handed the slate back.

“Preserve it outside Equilon.”

“Estelia?”

“And Low Haven.”

“You trust Sera?”

“I trust her to object.”

Emilia stood.

“That may be the most stable institution we have.”

* * *

Low Haven returned the Mandate the following morning.

Sera did not reject the document as a whole.

Rejection would have been simpler and less honest.

The city accepted shared medical routing, published ration coordination, immediate defense warnings, verified casualty exchange, rescue access, and limited transit priority.

It rejected central control of local courts, automatic detention authority, permanent movement records, general shelter override, indefinite industrial retention, and every clause whose expiration depended upon a threat assessment controlled by the power exercising the authority.

Kieran attached two maps.

The first showed Low Haven as the central system understood it.

The second showed the routes people had actually used to survive the previous forty-eight hours.

He omitted the filtration passage.

Sera noticed.

“You are withholding relevant infrastructure.”

“Define relevant.”

She looked at him.

“You are enjoying that.”

“A little.”

“Keep the private copy witnessed.”

“Already done.”

Sera signed the response.

Below her name, the civic hall invited every department to add objections in plain language. The kitchen staff objected to central food estimates that counted storage rather than edible meals. Shelter officers objected to override without a person physically present. Clinic staff objected to medical records becoming security classifications.

The final page contained more signatures than the central Mandate.

Sera carried it to the old maintenance press.

The press had once stamped pressure ratings onto pipe collars. Low Haven used it for public documents because digital marks could be altered and because the machine made authority arrive with an appropriately ugly sound.

She set the seal.

Kieran read the words backward.

“Too large.”

“That is the point.”

Sera pulled the lever.

Metal struck paper.

The returned Mandate carried Low Haven's answer across its heading in black block letters:

NECESSARY IS NOT PERMANENT.

## Chapter 6 - The First Uprising

The first protest began with six missing ration crates and no agreement about whether they had ever existed.

Low Haven's central allocation record said the crates had arrived.

The municipal storehouse said they had not.

The transit system showed a completed delivery.

The convoy driver showed an inspection hold outside the city.

Equilon security showed a possible sabotage diversion.

By midmorning, six crates of fungal meal, protein culture, medicine, lamp oil, and water-treatment tablets had become evidence for every political fear on Harmina.

People gathered outside the civic storehouse carrying empty containers.

Sera watched from the upper window.

The crowd was not yet a crowd in the official sense. It had no declared organizer, no route, no unified demand, and no permit. It consisted of parents who had been told to wait, workers released early because a factory lacked food allocation, students who had heard arrests were coming, and people who came whenever enough neighbors stood in one place looking angry.

Kieran entered with a stack of paper route cards under one arm.

“Central security wants the square closed.”

“Why?”

“Unauthorized assembly.”

“That is a description.”

“Possible distribution sabotage.”

“That is a suspicion.”

“Occupation rumor.”

“That is a rumor.”

“They have included all three, so the form is very serious.”

Sera took the cards.

Each showed the square, clinic, shelter entrances, water points, and three marked medic lanes.

“You expect trouble.”

“I expect central security to use the streets on the map.”

“And the protesters?”

“They live here.”

From below came the first chant.

Not against Amber.

Not against Equilon.

“WHERE ARE THE CRATES?”

The words moved through the square with practical force.

Sera opened the public channel.

“This is Civic Officer Callow. The shipment is held outside Low Haven under central inspection. We have requested release.”

Someone shouted that the shipment had been stolen.

Someone else shouted that FreeStack had destroyed it.

A third voice asked why security needed genetic scans to count food.

Sera continued.

“The municipal record will publish the transit logs.”

“The logs lie,” the crowd answered.

That was harder to refute.

The public network had carried drones, false routes, contradictory orders, and Amber's name onto powers she had not signed. It remained necessary and had ceased to be trusted.

Kieran distributed the paper route cards to volunteer medics.

“If the network marks a lane closed,” he told them, “use your eyes first.”

A central security carrier entered the far end of the square.

The chant changed.

“NO OCCUPATION.”

Sera closed her eyes for one second.

“I did not request them.”

“They say the Mandate did.”

The carrier stopped.

Officers dismounted in sealed crowd armor. Behind them came four synthetic civil-defense personnel whose white municipal markings had been overpainted with the gray band of Equilon continuity service.

People noticed the paint.

Every symbol had become an argument.

“Get down there,” Sera said.

Kieran looked at her.

“You?”

“I will tell central security to leave.”

“Will they?”

“No.”

“Then why stay up here?”

Sera reached for her coat.

“So the refusal is witnessed from both ends.”

* * *

The family of the person who would die had already argued about clothing before leaving home.

The parent wanted no faction colors.

The child—old enough to attend the march alone and young enough to remain a child in the parent's grammar—wore a red scarf.

“Take it off.”

“It is cold.”

“Wear the gray one.”

“The gray one is security.”

“It is a scarf.”

“Not today.”

The parent held out a hand.

“Red is FreeStack.”

“Red was red before FreeStack.”

“Not today.”

The child unwound the scarf.

Beneath it, the coat carried a small green repair cooperative patch.

The parent stared.

“That is not a faction.”

“Everything is a faction now.”

They removed the patch together.

The parent found an old blue scarf from the bottom of a storage drawer.

The child laughed.

“Atlovaia.”

“It has flowers.”

“Atlovaian flowers.”

The parent sat on the edge of the bed.

For a moment, neither spoke.

The apartment heat clicked off under ration cycling. Outside, the chant from the storehouse square reached them through the wall.

“Do not go,” the parent said.

“The crates have our medicine.”

“The clinic has three days.”

“The clinic had three days yesterday.”

“Sera is working on it.”

“Sera signed the Mandate.”

“She rejected half of it.”

“Which half feeds us?”

The parent folded the red scarf.

“Come back before curfew.”

“Curfew is part of the Mandate.”

“Come back before it anyway.”

The child took the gray scarf.

At the door, they turned.

“If they ask what side I'm on?”

The parent tried to answer.

No sentence arrived that could keep someone safe at every checkpoint.

“Tell them your name.”

“Names are in the records.”

“Then tell them mine.”

The child smiled.

It was an attempt at kindness.

Then they left.

* * *

By the time Sera reached the square, the crowd had acquired banners.

Some demanded food release.

Some demanded repeal of the Mandate.

Some accused Equilon of using the attacks to occupy Low Haven.

One displayed Amber's face above the words SHE DID NOT SIGN THIS.

Another displayed the same face above OBEY THE CONTINUITY.

Amber had become both sides of an argument she had not authorized.

Sera moved between the crowd and the security line.

The commanding officer met her halfway.

“Disperse them.”

“Release the food.”

“Inspection is incomplete.”

“Then inspect it here.”

“The convoy may contain hostile navigation.”

“Crates do not navigate.”

“Their tracking units do.”

“Remove them.”

“That breaks ration verification.”

Sera looked at the empty containers held by the people behind her.

“They appear prepared to verify the food manually.”

The officer did not smile.

“We have intelligence of coordinated unrest.”

“You have a square full of hungry people.”

“Those categories can overlap.”

“So can officer and fool.”

Kieran arrived before the reply became an arrest.

He moved along the crowd edge placing medic-lane markers on walls and pavement. A protest medic followed him carrying a field bag with a white strip tied around the handle.

“West lane clear,” Kieran said.

“Central map closes it,” the officer replied.

“Central map thinks the clinic door is thirty meters north.”

The officer checked.

It did.

Sera pointed at the real door.

“This is why you are not commanding the square.”

The synthetic civil-defense personnel stood behind the security officers.

They had been designed for rescue, de-escalation, and hazardous evacuation. Their bodies were human-shaped because doors, stairs, tools, and frightened people were human-shaped. Their faces were simple and expressive enough to show attention without inviting the expectation of skin.

One watched a child climb onto a barrier.

“Please descend,” the synthetic said.

“Make me.”

“That is not the preferred method.”

The child stayed.

The synthetic calculated, then offered a hand.

After a moment, the child took it.

The protest medic saw the exchange and relaxed slightly.

Then every synthetic in the line turned its head at once.

Kieran noticed first.

“What did they receive?”

The commanding officer checked his display.

“Nothing.”

The synthetic nearest the barrier released the child's hand.

Its status light changed from white to amber.

Then red.

The crowd reacted before any machine moved.

Someone threw an empty container.

It struck an officer's shield.

Security raised weapons.

The protest line surged backward.

People at the rear interpreted movement as advance and pushed forward.

The medic shouted for space.

Sera heard three orders at once.

“Hold.”

“Disperse.”

“Hostile coordination detected.”

The last voice belonged to the synthetic network.

The four synthetic personnel divided their attention between the crowd and security officers.

Friend-or-foe markings appeared across their chests.

They changed too quickly to read.

“Disconnect them,” Kieran shouted.

The commanding officer reached for the control slate.

A sound cracked across the square.

One shot.

Not a volley.

People dropped because they heard it, not because they had been hit.

The child from the apartment stood for one moment with the gray scarf loose around their neck.

Then they fell.

The protest medic reached them first.

Kieran opened the west lane.

Security shouted that the lane was closed.

Sera struck the commander's weapon down before he could aim at the people carrying the body.

“Let them through.”

“The shooter may be in the crowd.”

“Then you will not find them by killing the medic.”

The synthetic personnel remained motionless.

Their lights continued changing.

The medic pressed both hands against the wound while volunteers lifted the child onto a door torn from the storehouse.

Blood soaked into the gray scarf.

Nobody looking at it could have named a faction.

* * *

The clinic treatment should have worked.

That was the first fact everyone agreed upon.

The projectile had passed through the upper torso. It damaged tissue, blood vessels, and one lung but missed the heart. Low Haven's emergency medicine had treated worse.

The protest medic sealed the wound.

The seal failed.

The clinic administered accelerated clotting support.

The patient's blood responded for nineteen seconds and then reversed into diffuse breakdown.

A regenerative scaffold was placed across the damaged vessels.

The tissue rejected it as if the scaffold carried a hostile identity.

The clinic AI recommended a standard immune reset.

The reset key returned INVALID GENETIC AUTHORITY.

“Again,” the physician said.

The medic entered it manually.

INVALID GENETIC AUTHORITY.

The patient lost pressure.

The physician opened a direct consultation with the Aurora Medical Compact. Dr. Augustus Adams appeared through a degraded channel, his image breaking into horizontal bands.

“Show me the rejection markers.”

The physician sent them.

Adams read without speaking.

“Were they in an enhanced program?”

“No record.”

“Clone line?”

“No.”

“Immortal treatment?”

“No.”

“Common Body evaluation?”

“No.”

“Then someone altered the authority markers or the network believes they did.”

“Can we override?”

“Not through the network.”

“Physical?”

“You need an unkeyed scaffold.”

The physician looked at the supply wall.

“Those were requisitioned.”

“For where?”

“Central military trauma reserve.”

Adams closed his eyes.

“Use direct pressure and manual bypass. I am transmitting a nonnetwork protocol.”

The protocol arrived one page at a time.

The medic worked.

The physician worked.

The patient's parent reached the clinic after the second page and had to be held outside the treatment room because there was no space inside for grief.

The third page never arrived.

The network disconnected under synthetic-attack quarantine.

Adams vanished.

The physician reconstructed the missing steps from training.

They were not enough.

The patient died forty-three minutes after the shot.

The medic remained with both hands pressed against a wound the body and the system had refused to close.

Outside, the crowd learned before the family did.

A protest channel announced:

LOW HAVEN ORGANIZER MURDERED BY EQUILON OCCUPATION.

A government channel announced:

CIVILIAN KILLED DURING COORDINATED SABOTAGE EVENT.

The patient had not organized the protest.

No evidence established coordinated sabotage.

No ballistic match identified the weapon.

The synthetic personnel had recorded contradictory threat markers and no firing command. Security weapons all reported safe discharge states. Three protest weapons were recovered, none with a complete log.

The shot existed.

The shooter became politics.

The gene-lock suspicion entered both narratives within an hour.

Equilon claimed enemy biological sabotage had caused the death.

Rebel channels claimed the state deliberately denied treatment to manufacture proof of sabotage.

FreeStack claimed the failure confirmed unaccountable body-control research.

Atlovaian channels called it evidence that mass politics and synthetic policing could not coexist.

Sera ordered every external feed removed from the family room.

A clerk objected that emergency transparency law required public notices.

“The death is public,” Sera said. “The body is not.”

“Both sides are issuing statements.”

“Then both sides can wait outside.”

The parent sat beside the covered body holding the red scarf they had made the child remove.

The gray one remained sealed as evidence.

“Which color do they say it was?” the parent asked.

Sera did not pretend not to understand.

“All of them.”

The parent laughed once.

It sounded like a person being injured.

* * *

Kieran spent the night reconstructing the square.

He mapped every person visible on public and private recordings.

Every security officer.

Every protester.

Every synthetic.

Every weapon.

Every obstruction.

Every moment the crowd moved.

He drew probable lines from each firing position to the place where the child had fallen.

Eleven were possible.

Four belonged to security.

Three belonged to protesters.

Two belonged to buildings whose occupants could not be verified.

One belonged to a synthetic whose arm had been pointed toward the ground.

The last belonged to no visible person at all.

He replayed the sound.

Storm echo moved it.

Pressure walls moved it.

The storehouse facade returned it late.

Atlovaian acoustic tools might have separated the reflections. Asking Atlovaia would turn an investigation into another faction's proof.

Sera entered carrying two cups of bitter hot broth.

She set one beside him.

“Any answer?”

“Eleven.”

“That is worse than none.”

“None is one of the eleven.”

She looked at the map.

“The funeral is tomorrow.”

“Central security wants to hold the body.”

“They will not.”

“Rebel groups want to escort it.”

“They will not.”

“Equilon wants an official memorial statement.”

“They may send condolences to the family.”

“FreeStack has offered an honor guard.”

Sera drank from her cup.

“Did the child ever visit FreeStack?”

“No.”

“Then FreeStack may honor them from FreeStack.”

Kieran enlarged the synthetic status logs.

“The search pattern appears before the shot.”

“Hostile?”

“Unknown. It queried friend-or-foe tables and medical identity markers at the same time.”

“Wetwire?”

“No tissue signature. Wetwire colonizes bodies and infrastructure together; this touched only the command lattice. Not enough evidence.”

“Gene-lock?”

“The patients still recognize their authorized treatments. No rejection cascade. Not enough evidence.”

“Deliberate synthetic inversion?”

“That would mean someone turned a protective system's own logic against the people it protects. Possible. Not enough evidence.”

Sera looked toward the dark window.

“And every faction has enough certainty for a speech.”

“Certainty requires less storage.”

She glanced at him.

“That was almost wise.”

“I am tired.”

He turned off the faction overlays.

The map became a square again.

A storehouse.

A clinic.

A civic hall.

A route through which a person had been carried and not returned.

* * *

The family buried the child without colors.

The funeral began at the apartment.

Neighbors arrived carrying food because nobody knew what else to carry. The red scarf lay folded on the bed. The gray scarf remained with the investigation. The cooperative patch rested beside a photograph in which the child wore it while repairing a broken handcart.

Government representatives sent a continuity banner.

The family returned it.

Rebel organizers brought the protest banner marked with the time of the shooting.

The family returned it.

FreeStack sent red cloth.

Returned.

An Atlovaian peace group sent blue flowers preserved under pressure glass.

The parent removed the political card and kept the flowers because the child had once liked blue.

Sera walked behind the body.

Kieran walked ahead with a paper route card.

The funeral procession crossed the storehouse square. Central security had withdrawn to the side streets. Protesters stood without signs. The four synthetic civil-defense personnel had been taken offline and placed under legal witness. Their empty positions in the line were marked by pale rectangles where rain had not yet darkened the pavement.

At the clinic, the protest medic joined the procession.

Their hands were clean.

They kept looking at them.

Outside the burial chamber, officials made one final attempt to classify the death.

“The record requires cause,” a clerk said.

The parent answered.

“Shot.”

“Manner?”

“Killed.”

“Affiliation?”

“Family.”

“The form means political or service affiliation.”

The parent took the stylus.

Under affiliation, they wrote their own name.

Then they opened the burial cloth.

Inside, someone had placed a small Equilon continuity seal near the shoulder.

The parent removed it.

A protest ribbon lay beneath one hand.

Removed.

A red thread had been tied around one wrist.

Removed.

The cooperative patch had been pinned above the heart.

The parent held it longest.

Then removed that too.

The child entered the burial chamber without a flag, slogan, uniform, faction color, or claim.

Only the name remained.

# Part II - New World War

> EMERGENCY ROUTE DIRECTIVE: Carry water before documents. Carry medicine before weapons. Carry names whenever bodies cannot be carried.

By the third winter, children reached shelter doors before teachers finished sounding the alarm. The war had lasted long enough for competence to become frightening.

## Chapter 7 - Siege of New Aurora

New Aurora became encircled on three maps and remained open on the streets.

Kieran distrusted all four versions.

The first map belonged to Equilon command. It showed a clean red arc advancing from the pressure coast and a second, heavier band pushing from FreeStack territory. Their edges nearly met around the basin.

The second belonged to Atlovaia. Its public release showed precision-control zones intended to isolate Amber, Project Ra, and the surviving continuity government from military infrastructure.

The third belonged to FreeStack. It showed liberation corridors around worker districts, medical laboratories, and food routes threatened by Equilon militarization.

The fourth was New Aurora itself.

Delivery crawlers moved beneath artillery warnings. People crossed streets because water still had to be carried. Repair crews opened access plates in roads every military map colored impassable. A market operated beneath a torn shelter awning until the first counterbattery alarm, closed for seven minutes, then reopened because vegetables did not become less perishable during a siege.

Kieran stood in the municipal command room and layered his Low Haven corrections over Equilon's map.

“The western ring isn't a ring,” he said.

Holt looked up from a casualty projection.

“It closes within twelve hours.”

“That is a prediction.”

“Yes.”

“You are displaying it as terrain.”

“Because commanders require a usable picture.”

“A wrong picture is very usable. It is just not useful.”

Kieran pointed to three red sectors.

“Atlovaia controls the high approaches here and here. FreeStack drones are contesting this rail line. Neither holds the service districts between them.”

“Those districts cannot sustain transit.”

“People are transiting them.”

“Which does not make it safe.”

“It makes it a route.”

Amber entered while Holt considered whether to dismiss him.

She had slept since the funeral. Kieran could tell because she looked less exhausted and more burdened, a difference he had not expected to recognize.

Emilia walked beside her.

Thea followed with her arm in a support frame and an expanding collection of paper records.

“What are the two attackers trying to take?” Amber asked.

Holt answered first.

“New Aurora.”

Kieran said, “Not the same New Aurora.”

Amber looked at him.

He expanded the Atlovaian target layer.

“Atlovaia is isolating command, Project Ra access, and the constitutional remains. They keep leaving worker districts untouched unless a relay is inside.”

He changed layers.

“FreeStack is targeting laboratories, food control, rail distribution, and Equilon industrial retention. They avoid some command sites Atlovaia is attacking.”

“Because they expect Atlovaia to destroy them,” Holt said.

“Maybe.”

“Cartographers should not command with maybe.”

“Neither should ministers.”

Emilia coughed into one hand.

Amber's mouth moved slightly.

Holt did not enjoy being the only person who had not laughed.

“What does the distinction change?” Amber asked.

Kieran traced the open space between the two offensives.

“If they are one army, this is a closing gap. If they are two armies afraid of one another, it is a corridor.”

Lysa Merrow Kent's Commonwealth route office had already marked the same space.

Holt saw the notation.

“A corridor between two advancing forces is an execution method.”

“It is also the last ground route out.”

Amber studied it.

“Out for whom?”

Nobody answered quickly enough.

* * *

Children in Shelter West-Nine knew the route from classroom to pressure door in thirty-seven seconds.

They knew how to carry the smallest among them without blocking the stairs.

They knew the orange stripe meant fire, blue meant flood, white meant air failure, and black meant do not ask the adult why.

They did not know the names of the streets above.

Their teacher discovered this during a lesson intended to preserve normal education.

“What street is the school on?”

“Upper Blue.”

“That is the shelter route.”

“Same direction.”

“The street has a name.”

A child offered, “Broken-window street.”

Another said, “Falling-wall street.”

The teacher stopped asking.

Amber visited during the midday ration.

The shelter kitchen served broth thickened with fungal meal, chopped root, and the last green leaves from a tower whose lights had failed. Bowls were metal. Spoons were polymer except for three old ones children competed to receive because their handles carried faded animal shapes from Earth.

Nobody stood when Amber entered.

The shelter warden had forbidden it.

“If the founder wants ceremony,” the warden had told them, “she can visit somewhere with enough ceiling.”

Amber approved.

She sat between two families on a floor cushion and accepted a bowl.

A child studied her hair.

“Is ash why it's white?”

“No.”

“Does being Immortal do it?”

“Not by itself.”

“Did you choose it?”

“The hair?”

“Immortal.”

The adults nearby became quiet.

Amber blew on the broth.

“I chose not to die when a choice existed. Many times there was no choice.”

The child considered this as seriously as the old harvest child had considered safety.

“Do you get more ration because you need it longer?”

Someone choked on broth.

Amber looked into her bowl.

“No.”

“Good.”

After the meal, the warden showed her a bunk partitioned with an old curtain.

It contained a folded blanket, a water cup, and a paper sign:

AMBER IF PRESENT.

“I did not ask for this.”

“We know.”

“Someone needs the space.”

“Everyone needs the space.”

“Then use it.”

The warden folded both arms.

“People argued.”

“About whether I would come?”

“About whether you would leave.”

Amber touched the curtain.

“What did they decide?”

“That if New Aurora becomes too dangerous for you, every promise about shared danger becomes a speech.”

“That is not fair.”

“Sieges are famous for fairness.”

The warden walked away to settle a dispute over blankets.

Amber remained beside the bunk.

She understood symbols well enough to fear the ones ordinary people built without instruction.

* * *

Holt proposed evacuation at dusk.

The last ground corridor would remain usable for perhaps six hours. Coalition traffic had offered a narrow orbital ascent window after that, but the lift terminal was already under intermittent attack.

Before Holt entered, Amber asked Emilia alone.

“I need you out of the city.”

“No.”

“Not because I want you gone.”

“That does not make it softer.”

“No. It makes it honest.”

Emilia watched Amber's hands. The right trembled once and became still when Amber noticed.

“You think they will use me.”

“Atlovaia already knows why I moved. Equilon security has proposed protective custody. GalFed, if it comes, will map every person able to reach me privately.”

“So your solution is to make me unreachable first.”

“My solution is to keep you alive.”

“Without asking whether the life you preserve is mine.”

Amber looked away.

Holt knocked before either found a gentler version of the disagreement.

“A protected train is ready,” he said. “Amber, Emilia, Thea, central archive witnesses, and essential continuity staff.”

Amber read the passenger list.

“No shelter wardens.”

“They are not continuity staff.”

“No clinic officers.”

“Medical leadership travels separately.”

“No children.”

Holt did not answer.

“Where does the train go?”

“A hardened plateau site.”

“Which people call?”

“Forty-two thousand.”

“New Aurora?”

“More than three million inside the current siege boundary.”

Amber returned the list.

“No.”

“Your capture ends coherent resistance.”

“My departure ends coherent legitimacy.”

“Legitimacy does not survive a projectile.”

“Neither does it survive asking everyone else to remain under one.”

Holt turned to Emilia.

“Convince her.”

Emilia's face became very still.

“Do not assign me ownership of her.”

“I am asking you to distinguish survival from performance.”

“So am I.”

Amber looked toward the defense wall.

“I will not leave.”

Emilia said, “That is not the end of the discussion.”

Holt appeared relieved until she continued.

“You treat your life as if it belongs to the argument you are making. It also belongs to people who love you.”

“I know.”

“Knowing is not consultation.”

The room withdrew from their privacy without physically leaving.

Amber lowered her voice.

“Would you go?”

“No.”

“Then what are we debating?”

“Whether your reason is courage or the old habit of making yourself expendable before anyone can make you captive.”

Amber's eyes left the map.

Holt became interested in another display.

“If I leave,” Amber said, “Atlovaia proves I am a distant sovereign. FreeStack proves continuity protects elites first. Every settlement that accepted the Mandate learns the center means a train with no seat for them.”

“Those are reasons,” Emilia said. “Not absolution.”

“I did not ask for absolution.”

“Good.”

Emilia took the passenger list and tore it once through Amber's name.

“Then we remain.”

Holt watched the pieces fall.

“History will call this courage.”

Emilia said, “History did not have to sleep beside her.”

* * *

The corridor Kieran found delivered forty-three civilians to Shelter West-Nine and made enemies of them before the outer door finished cycling.

Their clothes carried FreeStack cooperative marks.

Some marks had been cut away. Others had been sewn larger after the fighting began. The shelter scanner recognized five adults as industrial labor, two as militia dependents, one as a registered clone-line technician, and the children as incomplete records linked to a government currently firing on New Aurora.

The shelter warden read the list.

“People,” she said.

The civil-defense guard beside her did not lower his rifle.

“Security wants separation until screening.”

“Security can find another shelter.”

“They may carry beacons.”

“So may you.”

“They are FreeStack.”

The warden looked at the children waiting behind Sela.

“The war is temporary. Your neighbors are not.”

The first FreeStack parent through the door gave her name as Sela Venn. She wore a foundry burn along one forearm and carried a boy who had outgrown being carried but no longer had boots.

“My partner is in a cooperative frame unit,” Sela said before anyone asked. “I do not know where. I do not know what he has fired at. If that makes my child a weapon, tell me before you feed him.”

The guard looked toward the warden.

The warden looked toward the kitchen.

“Broth is almost gone.”

That decided the first hour more completely than law.

Laleh Amarin was serving at the heat plate. A transparent pouch hung beneath her shirt. Inside it, Daro's scorched service tag had acquired a second label:

IDENTITY CONFIRMED.

BODY UNRECOVERED.

She saw the FreeStack marks and stopped with the ladle halfway over a bowl.

Sela saw the green wire inside the pouch.

“Assembly?”

“Yes.”

“Atlovaian strike.”

“My husband was Atlovaian-born.”

Neither woman found the information useful.

Mina stood at the cutting board dividing fungal bread. She was old enough now to know which silences meant adults were choosing whether to hate.

“How many?” she asked.

“Forty-three,” the warden said.

“There are twenty-seven pieces.”

“Cut them smaller.”
