Inside Artemis, eighty meters was the width between a nursery and vacuum.

The *Plain Hand* continued pushing.

Kilo rotated defensive guns toward it.

Nera locked the nearest docking lane.

The platform's targeting system interpreted the closure as collision authority and paused fire for automatic arbitration.

“How long?” the operator asked.

“Seven seconds.”

“Traffic law wins wars.”

“Maintenance wins traffic law. Push.”

The tug's drive temperature rose.

Five seconds.

Kilo challenged the lock.

Four.

Hardliner command attempted to revoke Nera's traffic credential.

Three.

Local Seal Authority had not yet been restored. Her access prompt changed from green to amber.

Two.

Nera placed her family-compartment seal beside the traffic credential. The station recognized her first as resident, second as operator, and last as a command subordinate.

The lock held.

One.

The *Plain Hand* moved Kilo beyond clean firing alignment.

Arbitration ended.

Kilo fired into empty orbit.

The operator laughed once, then stopped.

The tug drive had exceeded tolerance. Its return burn would fail unless another vessel recovered it.

“Status?” Nera asked.

“Correction complete.”

“Personal status.”

The operator looked at the warning.

“Invoice disputed.”

Nera assigned two rescue drones and one family shuttle whose pilot volunteered before military command could deny civilian involvement.

The shuttle pilot performed the airlock checklist over an open channel.

Destination.

Pressure.

Air.

Route.

Return.

“Intended,” the pilot said.

Across Artemis, station families answered:

“Witnessed.”

A boarding team entered through a waste port.

Hardliners sealed civilian sections to force surrender.

Ilyana restored Seal Authority locally, giving station technicians power above military command.

Doors opened.

Families moved.

The hardliners lost their leverage.

* * *

The first door opened onto a nursery.

Kieran had expected a weapons team. He found six children zipped into pressure cocoons while two adults held repair patches over a hairline crack in the outer wall. The compartment lights had gone amber. Every few seconds the ventilation fan lost pitch, caught again, and blew the smell of sealant and frightened bodies through the room.

One of the adults pointed a cutting tool at his chest.

“Coalition?”

“Map service.”

“That is not an allegiance.”

“Today it is.”

The adult did not lower the tool. Behind Kieran, the boarding team waited with their weapons against their shoulders and their suit lamps pointed at the floor. The hardliners had told the families that the coalition would open the station to vacuum rather than fight room by room. Coalition command had told the boarders that every closed compartment might contain an ambush.

Both claims now stood in the nursery, breathing the same thin air.

Kieran raised his empty hands. “Local Seal Authority is restored. You can close this door after we pass.”

“Can you override us?”

“Not without two station witnesses.”

“Can they?”

The adult indicated the armed boarders.

“Not legally.”

“I asked whether they can make you.”

Kieran looked back at the team leader.

She removed her override key from her wrist housing and placed it on the deck. The other boarders followed. Metal and ceramic clicked softly against the floor.

“Not physically either,” she said.

The cutting tool came down.

They crossed the nursery one at a time so the damaged compartment would never hold more mass than its repair brace could tolerate. A child watched Kieran’s boots.

“Are you taking Artemis?”

“We are stopping someone else from taking all of it.”

“That sounds like taking it.”

“It might.”

The child considered that with the grave suspicion station children gave any answer involving air.

“Then put it back when you’re done.”

Kieran promised to record the demand.

Beyond the nursery, the corridor divided around a spinning service hub. Hardliner fire came through the spoke in measured bursts. The boarders could not return fire without striking coolant lines behind the position.

Ilyana spoke through Kieran’s earpiece.

“Maintenance rotation changes in forty-one seconds.”

“Can you stop it?”

“Stopping it locks three pressure doors and cuts circulation to the nursery.”

“Then do not stop it.”

“I was not proposing that.”

The hub turned. For four seconds, its solid shielding crossed the hardliner position. The boarders moved behind the shielding, boots catching handholds, bodies briefly weightless as the station changed their direction of down. One soldier vomited into her helmet and kept moving. Kieran followed with his map projected inside his visor, the routes bending as Artemis rotated beneath him.

No surface plan had prepared them for a battlefield where gravity changed between doors and every missed shot remained in the home it failed to defend.

They reached the control spoke without firing.

The hardliners inside had sealed themselves behind an emergency bulkhead. Ilyana could open it. Doing so remotely would also flag her authority as a hostile intrusion and cause two medical compartments to reject all network commands.

“Local witness,” Kieran said.

A maintenance worker crawled out of an access trunk. She wore sleep clothes under a pressure hood and carried a wrench older than the station.

“I witness,” she said.

The door opened.

The hardliners surrendered after seeing that the coalition had not depressurized the nursery.

In the command record, the capture occupied three lines.

Kieran added the wrench.

* * *

Kieran found the private answer key.

It appeared as a routing exception inside public communication.

One faction could answer GalFed and make the reply appear planetary.

“This is a border,” he said.

Ilyana isolated it.

Deleting the key would disable emergency contact for two habitats.

They rewrote the route instead.

Artemis remained functional.

Coalition neutrality did not.

Then the external signal arrived.

It addressed:

THE PLANETARY GOVERNMENT OF HARMINA.

The answer field waited.

Harmina had captured the right to speak before deciding what it was.

* * *

The signal waited for six hours.

Every power drafted an answer.

Equilon identified the Covenant process.

The Coalition claimed Artemis held orbital contact authority.

FreeStack demanded the reply reject external registration.

Atlovaian remnants warned that silence might appear hostile.

Toldax proposed acknowledging receipt and nothing more.

Amber sat with station families before entering the answer chamber.

Tali showed her the flat picture of Harmina.

“Is this wrong?”

Amber turned it.

From one direction it was a world.

From another, a circle.

“It depends where you stand.”

“That is what adults say when both answers cause trouble.”

Nera hid a smile.

Amber asked what the family wanted GalFed told.

“That orbit is inhabited,” Nera said. “Not the sky above your government.”

Amber added the sentence.

The first response read:

Harmina receives the signal.

Its planetary government remains under witnessed formation.

Its surface, orbital, deep-region, synthetic, Immortal, enhanced, and local authorities speak through a provisional common channel.

No separate agreement may claim the whole.

Orbital homes are inhabited jurisdictions, not infrastructure.

Further exchange requires identification and peaceful approach.

The message revealed uncertainty.

Holt wanted stronger language.

Amber transmitted it unchanged.

GalFed replied with polite acknowledgment and a request for developmental records.

The first administrative demand arrived before the first envoy.

Ilyana remained in the answer chamber after the others left.

The room had been designed for crisis communication, not diplomacy. The chairs locked to the floor. Every surface could be wiped clean after pressure loss. A red handle above the console allowed a human operator to sever all incoming transmission physically. No one had pulled it since installation.

The GalFed request waited beneath Harmina’s response.

DEVELOPMENTAL RECORDS.

The category opened into population, governance maturity, conflict history, synthetic integration, biological modification, orbital capacity, defense coordination, and resource distribution.

Kieran read over her shoulder.

“They asked for our census, courts, bodies, weapons, and shortages.”

“Politely.”

“Does politeness reduce the request?”

“It reduces the chance that frightened people recognize it as reconnaissance.”

The Serrins arrived to reclaim the chamber for its ordinary night use. In peacetime, the room doubled as a pressure refuge. Nera unfolded sleeping cocoons while Tali’s flat drawing of Harmina remained attached beside the planetary reply.

“Are they coming?” Tali asked.

Ilyana looked at the approach estimates. “Something is.”

“Are they enemies?”

“We do not know.”

“Then why did everyone fight over who gets to talk to them?”

Kieran leaned against the answer console. “Because adults are afraid that the first voice heard becomes the owner of the conversation.”

Tali frowned. “Conversations don’t have owners.”

“Remember that,” Ilyana said.

She did not answer the developmental request.

Instead, she printed it.

The printer was a maintenance relic used when networks could not be trusted. It produced a narrow strip of physical film, warm at the edges. Ilyana signed the time of receipt. Kieran signed that no response had been sent. Nera signed that the request existed before any government decided how to describe it.

Three witnesses.

Then Ilyana placed the film inside the emergency case beside the red handle.

For the first night of official contact, Harmina’s most important diplomatic document slept in a pressure refuge with a family.

## Chapter 32 - Shadows of Craterheart

Craterheart began where surface maps ended.

Tomas descended by heat lift with Adams, Yara, engineers, and survey soldiers.

The hollow crust contained mines, reactor channels, geothermal arteries, ancient voids, and ecosystems described as geology because law protected life more than rock.

Workers ate from heatproof tins while the floor vibrated.

One survey soldier said, “If we call it habitat, command needs permission.”

Tomas felt movement beneath the bench.

“What is it?”

“Unknown.”

“Large?”

“That is the known part.”

The deep-region engineer placed one palm on the floor.

“Surface people call this infrastructure.”

Tomas copied the gesture. Vibration entered through his armor interface and became the phantom sense of something breathing beneath him.

“What do you call it?”

“Work.”

“That is a task. Name the thing.”

“It is the answer that keeps command from asking whether the thing we are mining has rights.”

The survey soldier stopped eating.

“We have never observed cognition.”

“We have never observed the whole body.”

Adams asked, “If this is habitat, what have the geothermal works done?”

The engineer looked at the heat pipes disappearing into stone.

“What settlements always do. Survive first. Name the damage after.”

The floor moved again.

Nobody finished the meal.

* * *

The heat lift took forty-three minutes to descend.

Its cage was open on three sides because enclosed cars trapped too much geothermal heat. Stone moved past close enough to touch. Mineral water ran down the shaft in silver threads and vanished into darkness below. Every few hundred meters, the lift crossed a settlement level: doors cut into pressure stone, laundry drying beside insulated pipes, children waiting behind safety rails to see soldiers go deeper than their parents wanted them to.

At the seventh level, the lift stopped for a maintenance crew.

An old worker entered with a case of bearing tools and no weapon. He looked at Tomas’s armor, then at the soldiers surrounding him.

“You’re heavy.”

“The lift rating allows us.”

“The rating assumes the shaft is not being shelled.”

Tomas checked the load display. “What should we leave?”

The soldiers looked at ammunition, water, medical packs, and one another.

The worker tapped the decorative shoulder plate on Tomas’s armor.

“That.”

Tomas detached it.

Two soldiers removed spare armor. Adams shifted a treatment case into her lap. The load fell below the worker’s preferred margin.

“Better,” he said.

“Will it matter?”

“Everything matters before failure. After failure, people call it fate.”

At the ninth level they passed a school assembled inside an abandoned ore chamber. The students had drawn the deep regions as branching colored lines. Surface maps rendered the same place as solid rock.

Yara pressed her face close to the cage mesh.

“Do those routes exist in the official survey?”

The worker laughed once. “Official survey knows the routes it taxes.”

“And the others?”

“The routes children use.”

Yara recorded the answer. The worker noticed.

“Do not publish the school tunnel.”

“Why?”

“Because armies read archives.”

She stopped the recording and marked the location sealed.

Tomas watched her do it. “A record no one can read.”

“A record someone must earn the right to read.”

The lift continued down.

By the bottom, sweat had filled the seams of Tomas’s armor. His interface cooled the machinery before it cooled him. The worker reclaimed the shoulder plate, turned it over, and wrote the lift’s load limit on the inside with a grease pencil.

“For the way back.”

Tomas did not tell him that military plans had not guaranteed one.

* * *

The ambush used mining drills as weapons.

The first drill entered the passage without an operator.

It came through the stone beside the coalition column, cutter head turning slowly enough to look almost harmless. Mining paint covered its chassis. A child's handprint remained beneath the dust where settlement schools had once toured the equipment.

Then the cutter accelerated.

The lead soldier fired.

Rounds flattened against the housing and ricocheted into heat pipes.

“Stop firing!” the deep-region engineer shouted.

The drill opened its slurry nozzles.

Pressurized mineral waste struck armor, filled optics, and hardened in cooling joints. Two soldiers fell without wounds because their suits could no longer bend.

Tomas engaged the drill with both hands.

Its cutter teeth caught his forearm plate.

The interface converted vibration into pain before suppression systems lowered it. For one second he felt the machine trying to turn him into workable ground.

He pushed sideways.

The drill's anchoring feet unfolded into the passage wall. Stone cracked around them.

“It is tied to the vent structure,” the engineer warned. “Pull it free and pressure moves.”

“How much?”

“Enough.”

Military language used numbers.

Deep-region workers used *enough* when numbers encouraged people to believe a limit was negotiable.

Tomas stopped pushing.

The cutter continued.

He opened a maintenance panel with the edge of his gauntlet and found civilian controls replaced by a remote command lattice. The attack code recognized his armor as obstruction and increased torque.

“Manual isolation?”

“Behind the cutter.”

“Of course.”

Tomas let the teeth close around his arm plate, rotated with them, and used the movement to pull himself across the machine. Warning lights filled his vision. The armor proposed breaking the anchoring feet.

He rejected it.

The machine drove him against the wall.

The deep-region engineer crawled beneath its rear housing with a physical shutdown wedge.

Enemy fire entered from the far passage.

Coalition soldiers returned it until a heat pipe split above them.

Steam did not erupt. Craterheart pressure held it inside the wall, turning stone dark as temperature climbed.

“Three minutes to binder failure,” the engineer said.

Tomas reached the manual lever.

It had been welded.

“Two minutes if the weld transfers heat.”

He closed his hand around the lever.

The armor insulated him.

The interface did not.

Heat appeared inside his palm as white sensation. He pulled until the weld stretched. The drill torque changed. Cutter teeth stopped against his elbow.

The engineer drove in the wedge.

The machine died.

Tomas remained pinned beneath it while infantry fought around the chassis.

“Advance,” command ordered.

“Extract first,” he answered.

“Rear unit will recover you.”

Tomas looked at the darkening wall.

“Recover the pipe.”

The deep-region engineer was already opening repair compound.

Two soldiers stayed.

One wore coalition markings.

The other had emerged from behind the enemy barricade with both hands raised and an engineer's heat key clipped to the chest.

“They locked us in when we refused the drill conversion,” the enemy engineer said.

The coalition soldier kept a weapon trained.

“Can you stabilize the pipe?”

“If your friend stops pointing that at the hand I need.”

The weapon lowered by centimeters.

Together the engineers opened the service wall.

The enemy knew which return valve had failed.

The deep-region worker knew where settlement crews had added an unauthorized bypass after the official plan was archived.

Tomas held the drill chassis off both while they connected the two versions of the system.

Pressure fell.

Temperature slowed.

Binder failure remained possible.

“Command wants us forward,” the coalition soldier said.

The enemy engineer looked toward the gunfire.

“Your command or mine?”

“Does it matter?”

“It mattered before the pipe.”

The deep-region engineer pushed repair compound into the split.

“The pipe has no government.”

The phrase did not make anyone friends.

It gave them ninety seconds of shared work.

Beyond the dead drill, infantry continued fighting through tunnels where one bad shot could release heat sufficient to kill both sides. The repaired vent held.

Then the titan moved.

The first movement felt like another drill.

Low vibration entered Tomas's boots and climbed through his armor. Targeting marked the passage wall as an approaching machine because the system had no hostile category large enough for the wall itself.

The enemy engineer stopped working.

“That ridge has never moved.”

The deep-region engineer placed a bare hand against the repaired pipe.

“It has. We called it settling.”

The passage narrowed.

Not from collapse.

The stone withdrew on one side and advanced on the other, preserving its shape as it changed location. Mineral veins bent without breaking. Heat channels shifted around the movement like blood avoiding pressure.

The soldiers in the adjoining tunnel stopped shooting one by one.

No ceasefire order had reached them.

Their sights had turned toward the same impossible motion.

Rock folded.

Heat changed direction.

A structure the surveys had called a mantle ridge flexed like muscle.

Weapons targeted it.

The engineer screamed for ceasefire.

Nobody knew whether the movement was migration, pain, attack, or the geological effect of a living body too large for military categories.

Tomas shut down his unit's targeting.

Others followed because the tunnel remained standing.

* * *

The hidden cache opened under an external-threat code.

Biological countermeasures.

Hardened synthetic substrates.

Unknown-environment medicine.

Radiation and gravity adaptations.

Weapons stores.

The same blank enemy field from Ilyana's prologue record.

Yara dated the cache before the Founder Session.

Orren received the evidence.

Malrec ordered the chamber destroyed to prevent Equilon control.

“It was built for an enemy outside Harmina,” Orren said.

“It was built beneath Amber's system.”

“Before the Clause.”

“Preparation proves intent.”

“It proves fear.”

Orren refused the destruction order.

The command that had fired on the Assembly finally broke over evidence the Assembly had never known.

* * *

The titan's movement trapped both forces below the cache.

Rock closed the primary lift.

Heat cut the eastern route.

Enemy and coalition survivors gathered in a cavern whose walls pulsed at intervals too regular for geology and too slow for ordinary life.

The engineer ordered weapons powered down.

One commander refused.

“We remain in combat.”

The wall moved.

Dust fell in a line across the commander's boots.

They powered down.

Adams treated a soldier from the opposing force using supplies from the hidden cache. The medicine had been designed for unknown biology. Its interface asked species, gravity history, atmospheric dependence, and cognitive substrate.

“No Harminan clinic asks cognitive substrate,” Adams said.

Yara copied the field.

“The builders expected synthetic or nonhuman patients.”

Tomas felt the titan through his armor.

It responded to reactor heat, retreating from high output and approaching low-frequency vibration. The engineers tested gentle pulses through mining anchors.

The wall relaxed.

“Communication?” a soldier asked.

“Response,” the engineer said. “Do not promote it to language because you want a conversation.”

They used vibration to guide the organism away from the blocked lift.

The route reopened.

No one knew whether they had communicated, conditioned, frightened, or merely coincided with its movement.

Yara preserved every interpretation.

On the surface, command wanted the cache secured.

The engineer wanted Craterheart closed to armies.

Tomas wanted the treatment stores used for people already altered by war.

Orren wanted the external-threat evidence released.

Amber ordered a joint scientific, medical, and legal custody rather than military ownership.

The cache remained guarded.

So did the unanswered question beneath it.

The trapped forces spent nine hours waiting for the reopened route to cool.

They made a clinic beneath the cache door. Coalition and enemy blankets divided the stone at first. Adams pulled them together because the medicine cases were easier to reach from one aisle.

“Keep your side,” an enemy sergeant told a wounded coalition soldier.

The soldier had a burn across half his face. “Which half of the floor is mine?”

Neither could point to a boundary that had survived the rockfall.

Adams treated them in injury order. The unknown-environment system proposed three interventions for the sergeant and refused to recommend one until he answered whether his modifications were inherited, acquired, or synthetic.

He stared at the choices.

“Military.”

“That is not a biological category,” Adams said.

“It was when they changed me.”

She selected acquired, then paused before confirmation. “Do you consent?”

“Will I die if I say no?”

“Possibly.”

“Then that is not consent.”

“No. It is still the only question I have.”

The sergeant looked toward his commander. The commander had no legal authority over coalition medicine and too much habit to remain silent.

Tomas stepped between them.

“His body. His answer.”

The sergeant selected yes himself.

When the medicine entered, every person nearby watched for rejection. His breathing slowed. The skin around the injection site remained unchanged.

“Did the outside enemy make this?” one soldier asked.

Yara sat amid copied labels and old procurement marks. “No. Harminans made it because they expected an outside enemy.”

“How long have we known?”

“That someone feared one? A long time.”

“That one exists?”

Yara looked up at the stone ceiling, as if orbit could be seen through it.

“Those are different sentences.”

The low-frequency pulses continued through the mining anchors. Each time they sounded, the cavern wall withdrew by a fraction. Workers began counting the intervals. Children in the settlement above later turned the count into a clapping game, though no one in the clinic knew that yet.

Tomas moved among the wounded. Without his decorative shoulder plate, one side of his armor looked unfinished.

The lift worker found him near the end of the wait.

“You left something.”

He held out the plate.

“Keep it,” Tomas said.

“Why?”

“The lift carried more medicine because it stayed behind.”

The worker examined the polished insignia and the grease-pencil number beneath it.

“Then it is useful now.”

He hung it above the clinic entrance as a load-limit sign.

When the route reopened, commanders tried to separate the forces again. The wounded refused to move until the medicine schedule had been copied for both sides.

The first agreement beneath Craterheart was not a treaty.

It was a dosage table.

## Chapter 33 - Breaking the Spires

The spire carried five kinds of safety and one enemy command.

Weather observation.

Defense calibration.

Communications.

Sanctuary signals.

External skywatch.

Hardliners had placed their command system inside all five.

Kieran walked beneath it with residents who used the spire to schedule births, memorial lights, storm warnings, and public meals.

An old couple showed him the light code from their wedding.

Three short pulses.

One long.

The pattern had crossed the district every anniversary until the war reassigned ceremonial bandwidth.

“When it falls,” one asked, “where does the marriage go?”

“The archive.”

“The archive did not see it.”

Kieran looked up at the structure.

Military maps divided the spire into command, communications, load, and target vulnerability.

Residents divided it into storms survived and people remembered.

He recorded the wedding pattern on his private map.

“If the strike happens, the archive will know.”

The couple thanked him.

The gesture did nothing to preserve the spire and prevented one small history from falling with it.

“Command calls it a target,” he said.

An elder touched the foundation.

“Command does not live under the shadow.”

Ilyana proposed isolating military functions.

Irena said the campaign would fail before the slow operation succeeded.

Amber asked for the price of each.

Slow capture: more soldiers, uncertain success, external eye preserved.

Immediate strike: enemy command destroyed, civilian district damaged, skywatch blinded.

No clean language.

* * *

The climbers entered through a maintenance door disguised as part of the foundation.

Kieran went with them because the acoustic codes did not describe what residents had added over three generations. The official plans showed a clear service shaft. The real shaft held memorial plaques, unauthorized cable bundles, a shelf of storm candles, and a narrow box where local children left questions for weather technicians.

Why does lightning choose?

Can the spire see my grandmother?

Where do clouds go when the warning ends?

The lead climber moved the box carefully.

“We have four minutes to the first military relay,” she said.

“Put it somewhere safe.”

“There is no safe in a target.”

Kieran clipped the box to his harness.

They climbed inside the spire’s vibration. Wind moved the outer structure until the ladder pressed against their gloves, released, and pressed again. Above them, city-scale aircraft made thunder too regular to be weather. Below, artillery flashes reflected in evacuation windows.

At the first relay, an Atlovaian code opened the maintenance lock.

At the second, it failed.

Orren’s voice reached them through static. “They changed the sequence after the Assembly strike.”

The climber looked at Kieran.

“Can you map another route?”

“Not without going outside.”

The exterior ladder had not been inspected since the war began. Frost covered its upper rungs. Kieran transferred the children’s question box to the climber.

“If I fall—”

“Do not make me archive your final sentence.”

He went outside.

The city appeared beneath him as light, smoke, and shelter marks. The spire’s curve hid its own summit. For several seconds, there was no visible structure above, only Kieran’s gloved hands on a ladder attached to the sky.

He moved one rung at a time.

The weather relay fired while he crossed. Its beam was invisible, but the suit alarm painted his visor red and the metal beneath his hands warmed.

“Kieran,” Ilyana said.

“Still attached.”

“Your suit dose is climbing.”

“So am I.”

“That was not wit.”

“It was all I had available.”

He reached the bypass panel, opened it, and found three manual switches labeled in a local repair shorthand absent from every military plan.

The old couple answered his call from the shelter.

“Blue, white, empty,” one said.

“There are two empty.”

“The one with wax in the hinge.”

Storm candles. Unauthorized maintenance. A detail command would have removed became the only reliable identification.

Kieran threw the switch.

Inside, the climbers passed the relay.

They gained two minutes.

* * *

Orren supplied Atlovaian acoustic codes.

Former enemies climbed the spire while city-scale aircraft fought above it and infantry fought below.

The command network used tether motion, relay beams, weather systems, and sanctuary beacons as countermeasures.

One beacon began broadcasting false evacuation routes.

Irena watched units move toward an ambush.

“Strike.”

Ilyana asked for six more minutes.

The request crossed three command systems before reaching Amber.

Irena's display showed units already entering the false corridor. Blue markers changed direction in disciplined groups, obeying sanctuary guidance designed to mean that someone had checked the road ahead. Hardliner guns waited behind the buildings toward which the beacon directed them.

Ilyana's display showed something else.

The climbers had reached the weather-control ring. If they could sever the command layer without killing the relay beneath it, the spire might retain storm warning and external observation. The operation had begun as a possibility measured in forty minutes. Combat had reduced it to six.

“What happens in six?” Amber asked.

“We isolate the sanctuary bus.”

Irena said, “Or lose the force following it.”

“Can you warn them?”

“The beacon overrides field traffic. We can jam it, but that also removes their navigation inside the smoke.”

“Runners?”

“Too far.”

“Local bells?”

Kieran answered from the spire.

“The district uses three descending tones for false shelter. Military units will not know it.”

“Teach them.”

He opened the common channel and struck the maintenance rail with a tool.

Three descending notes entered the spire.

Below, residents heard the pattern and began ringing hand bells from shelter doors. Soldiers heard only noise until a local route keeper ran into the street, arms spread, and pointed away from the beacon.

The lead unit stopped.

The second did not.

Hardliner fire crossed the avenue.

The route keeper disappeared inside dust.

Irena's casualty board added six names and one unknown civilian.

“Four minutes,” Ilyana said.

Inside the spire, the climbers reached the sanctuary bus.

It was not one cable.

Three generations of repairs had braided weather, memorial, public address, and military overrides through the same trunk. Cutting the red military line also killed the district pressure alarm. Cutting the black command line disabled birth and death notices. The original labels had peeled from heat.

“Which?” the lead climber asked.

Kieran opened the resident archive.

No schematic matched.

He called the old couple.

“When did the anniversary code stop?”

“Six years ago.”

“Which repairs happened then?”

One spouse answered immediately. “Lightning strike. West face.”

The other corrected, “That was the year before.”

“No, because Neri was born during the outage.”

“Neri is seven.”

“Neri says she is eight.”

“Children round upward.”

Above them, the relay fired again.

The climber's timer crossed three minutes.

Kieran closed his eyes and listened to the argument because the ordinary dispute contained the only chronology the war had not corrupted.

“The wax changed color after the repair,” one spouse said at last. “White before. Blue after.”

The climber found blue wax around one connector.

She pulled.

The false beacon stuttered.

For two seconds, the sanctuary route displayed both truth and ambush.

The lead ground unit turned.

Hardliner guns fired anyway.

“Two minutes,” Ilyana said.

Irena watched another blue marker stop moving.

“We do not have two.”

Amber heard Kieran breathing through the open channel. Metal struck his suit. Someone above him shouted that the tether brake had failed.

“Ilyana?”

“Isolation is possible.”

“Probability?”

“Fifty-eight percent.”

“Irena?”

“The strike ends command fire. Now.”

Amber's hand remained over authorization while a route keeper's body lay somewhere beneath the false sanctuary light.

“One minute,” she said.

Irena's jaw tightened.

She gave the ground force the minute without pretending it was free.

The hardliners began firing through the weather relay.

Irena authorized.

The spire broke halfway up.

Its upper section fell outside the densest district because climbers shifted the tether before impact.

People emerged from shelters to a skyline that had organized generations of memory and now ended in smoke.

Enemy command stopped.

On Ilyana's sky map, one sector turned black.

Harmina won the ground and lost an eye.

* * *

The falling section took twenty-six seconds to reach the unoccupied industrial basin.

For the people beneath it, the descent lasted long enough to contain several lives.

The climbers felt the tether shift and knew the strike had been authorized. They released the last counterweight manually. One remained above the break with a jammed harness.

Kieran crawled back toward her while the spire leaned.

“Leave it,” she said.

He cut the harness instead.

The ladder turned under them. Their safety line pulled horizontal. Wind, smoke, and pieces of the outer shell moved past in the wrong direction.

The climber grabbed Kieran’s wrist.

“Question box.”

He saw it tumbling from her harness.

For one reflexive instant he reached.

Then he caught the climber with both hands.

The box fell with the upper spire.

They descended inside the surviving section after impact. Every rung shook. Dust turned their lamps into small white walls.

At the foundation, residents waited for casualty names before asking about victory. Kieran reported the climbers. The old couple reported the wedding pattern. A child asked whether the weather technicians had answered the questions before the box was lost.

“No,” Kieran said.

“Then we have to ask again.”

The child found a clean sheet and began writing.

The first question was not about lightning.

Why did you break it?

Kieran carried that one to Irena himself.

The old couple returned to the spire foundation after shelters opened.

One carried a hand lamp.

The other carried the wedding code Kieran had archived.

The public network no longer reached the broken upper structure. They placed lamps along the fallen shadow instead.

Three short.

One long.

Residents joined.

Birth codes.

Memorial codes.

Storm-survival patterns.

The district rebuilt its sky in lights at ground level.

Irena arrived without escort.

The couple recognized her as the commander who authorized the strike.

“Was it necessary?” one asked.

She could have cited the false evacuation routes, command fire, and campaign clock.

“I believed it was.”

“Do you still?”

Irena looked at the broken eye above them.

“I do not know what the slower operation would have cost.”

“We know what this did.”

They handed her a lamp.

Not forgiveness.

Work.

Ilyana mapped the blind sector. GalFed approach probability had increased while skywatch weakened.

Kieran added the ground-light ritual to the map.

“That does not restore observation,” Ilyana said.

“No.”

“Then why?”

“The spire watched more than the sky.”

The next weather warning reached the district through runners, hand bells, and lamps.

Slower.

Less precise.

Still theirs.

* * *

The storm arrived before replacement sensors.

Pressure fell during the second night after the strike. Without the upper spire, the district received only twelve minutes of warning from a neighboring sector whose instruments saw the weather after the mountains had already changed it.

The old warning system had reached every apartment.

The new one required people.

Children carried lamps to corners where runners could see them. Maintenance crews climbed roofs and tied colored fabric to vents. The old couple stood at the foundation with the hand bell because their window faced the western cloud.

Kieran occupied the remaining control room halfway up the broken spire.

Rain entered through a temporary seal and moved across the floor in thin lines. The surviving displays showed an incomplete sky. Wind speed arrived from three borrowed stations. Lightning distance came from counting the gap between light and sound.

“That is not calibrated,” Ilyana said over the link.

“Neither is the storm.”

“Weather retains physics even when offended.”

“Distance nine.”

Thunder reached them.

Ilyana counted too.

“Eight point seven.”

“Put it in the public warning.”

“Confidence?”

Kieran looked down through the broken structure. Ground lamps formed the old weather code. Three short pulses moved from the western edge toward the shelter stairs.

“Witnessed.”

Ilyana released the warning.

Residents closed shutters. Street kitchens banked heat. Clinic staff moved patients away from the west walls. A parent on the seventh level found a child still writing questions for weather technicians and carried both child and paper to shelter.

The first gust struck.

The surviving spire flexed.

Everyone inside stopped talking to hear whether it would remain a structure.

It did.

The district lost two roof panels, one relay mast, and a row of public lamps. No shelter missed warning.

At dawn, Irena returned the borrowed hand lamp to the old couple.

Its casing had cracked.

“Cause?” one asked.

“I dropped it during the storm.”

“Negligent handling.”

Irena signed the repair tag.

The old couple hung it beneath the wedding code.

The military report recorded successful emergency weather continuity.

The district remembered a commander standing in rain, holding a broken lamp under a sky she had helped blind.

## Chapter 34 - Siege of Harmina

By the seventeenth year, the war possessed recruits who had been born after the Assembly Strike. Their induction forms asked whether they had prior combat exposure. Shelter duty, route fire, corrupted schools, and growing beneath artillery did not count.

Amber's operations wall stopped displaying nations.

Artemis.

Craterheart.

Spire sectors.

Rail.

Ports.

Cities.

Orbital defense.

External approach.

Harmina appeared as one contested object.

The image made unified command possible and every local life smaller.

“Restore civilian layers,” Amber said.

Holt answered, “The display cannot hold them at this scale.”

“Then change the scale.”

Emilia stood behind Amber while the operations wall enlarged cities until individual shelters appeared.

The commanders complained the display could no longer show every front.

“That is how people live,” Emilia said. “Unable to see every front.”

Amber selected four recurring locations rather than the whole planet.

Lysa's train.

Orren's submarine group.

Kieran's aerial city.

Tomas at Craterheart.

Four people could not represent Harmina.

They could prevent the map from becoming only geometry.

Emilia crossed the corridor to the Hall of Service mirror before the command vote.

The operations room displayed four living markers.

The Hall displayed the people around them.

Lysa's train expanded into mechanics, cooks, medics, drivers, route witnesses, rear guards, and families listed by carriage.

Orren's submarine group expanded into pressure crews, acoustic readers, divers, port tenders, and the civilians sleeping above evacuation harbors.

Kieran's aerial city expanded into school rosters, garden shifts, clinic beds, engine crews, and children whose identification had been written on their arms before acceleration.

Tomas at Craterheart expanded into geothermal workers, lift operators, enhanced soldiers, enemy prisoners, surface settlements, and maintenance patients dependent on the junction's power.

Mina Amarin was entering names from a route station destroyed before its last roster synchronized.

“How many?” Emilia asked.

“On the wall?”

“In the decision.”

Mina looked at the operation summary.

“The wall cannot count that.”

She handed Emilia four scorched tags recovered from a train carriage. Heat had fused two together. The route ledger identified one Commonwealth medic, one FreeStack child, one Equilon signal worker, and a fourth person whose government remained unknown.

“Command wants representative lives,” Mina said. “Take lives that refuse to represent only one thing.”

Emilia carried the tags back.

She placed one beside each human marker.

No commander asked whether the dead had tactical relevance.

“If one must be sacrificed?” Holt asked.

Amber looked at Emilia.

“Then use the word.”

Holt did.

The command room changed when sacrifice stopped being called resource prioritization.

Kieran divided the planet into routes rather than territories.

Lysa's megatrain convoy moved through artillery and storm-cut passes.

She abandoned a strategic junction to evacuate civilians from a settlement both sides had used as supply.

Command warned that the enemy would take it.

“After the people leave,” she said.

The enemy did.

The emerging state lost ground and gained citizens who had expected sacrifice.

* * *

The evacuation train stopped three kilometers beyond the junction because an axle sensor had overheated.

For twelve minutes, the war became a repair job.

Lysa lay beneath the second carriage with a rail mechanic while shells struck the abandoned switch behind them. Dust sifted from the suspension. Above, evacuees had been ordered not to move, which meant every frightened shift of weight changed the clearance around her ribs.

“Everyone left?” the mechanic asked.

“From the settlement.”

“The rear guard.”

Lysa thought of the rear guard and the two freight cars carrying false mass toward enemy targeting.

“No.”

The mechanic wiped grease across the sensor plate. “Then do not say evacuated in the report.”

“What should I say?”

“Say who is still there.”

The overheated component would not reset. The mechanic disconnected it, wrapped the wire around his thumb, and instructed Lysa to call speed changes by hand. She crawled out, climbed to the external platform, and spent the next hour watching the axle.

Families crowded the windows. One child held up a cup each time Lysa looked back, asking without opening the glass whether she needed water.

She always shook her head.

At the first safe tunnel, the child met her on the platform and handed her the cup anyway.

“My mother says people lie when they are working.”

Lysa drank.

Inside the carriage, settlement residents had arranged themselves by street. They argued over sleeping space, missing tools, and whether leaving counted as surrender. An elderly shopkeeper had brought the key to a door that no longer protected anything.

“Will we return?” he asked.

Lysa could have promised military recovery.

“I do not know.”

He closed his fist around the key. “Then why keep this?”

“Because not knowing is not the same as never.”

The mechanic entered the casualty record from his wrist slate.

JUNCTION YIELDED.

SETTLEMENT POPULATION REMOVED.

REAR GUARD STATUS UNKNOWN.

He showed it to Lysa before transmitting.

No one disappeared into the word evacuated.

* * *

In the Vermilion Deeps, Orren guided Atlovaian and Equilon submarines through leviathan water.

A hardliner boat carried strike weapons toward coastal evacuation ports.

Former enemies shared passive acoustic tracks.

The leviathans attacked active sonar, pressure wakes, and weapons discharge without caring which flag produced them.

The command deck used red light because white reflected from instrument glass and taught tired eyes to see shapes where none existed.

Orren sat beneath headphones connected directly to the hull.

At that depth, water carried the planet through metal.

Thermal vents clicked as they expanded. Distant shelf fractures rolled through the sea like doors closing in another building. The Equilon boat ahead drove with a slightly damaged bearing, a repeating scrape every eleven turns. The hardliner submarine sounded cleaner because it had left port after killing the crew who refused its order.

The leviathan sounded like weather deciding to breathe.

Its pulse entered below hearing. Cups trembled against restraints. Scar tissue in Orren's ribs tightened. One young Equilon analyst pressed a palm to a tooth that had begun aching when the creature turned.

“Range?” Orren asked.

“Unknown.”

“Direction?”

“Every return is water.”

The analyst's training expected geometry.

The animal occupied enough sea to make geometry feel presumptuous.

They hunted the hardliner by absence. The enemy captain had reduced propulsion and entered a cold current moving toward the ports. A conventional passive track lost the boat among vent noise. Orren instead watched the evacuation buoys.

One stopped transmitting.

Then another.

The hardliner was cutting them to blind coastal traffic.

“Course six degrees down-current.”

The Equilon captain answered from the other boat.

“That enters nesting depth.”

“It is already there.”

“Your evidence?”

“The buoys.”

“Could be the animal.”

“The animal does not remove antennae before crushing the housing.”

Silence.

The Equilon captain changed course.

Neither commander noted that trust had occurred. They were too occupied surviving it.

The two pursuit boats descended until pressure seals began reporting the differences between calculated strength and metal that had lived a long war. Crew spoke by touch and hand signal. Active sonar would find the enemy. It would also tell the leviathan where every submarine hid.

The hardliner fired first.

The weapon did not target them.

It targeted an evacuation port through a rising-water launch corridor.

The torpedo's drive entered the sea as a scream.

The leviathan turned.

Its movement displaced enough water to roll the Atlovaian boat thirty degrees. Orren's restraint caught across his chest. Tools tore loose despite nets. A maintenance case crossed the deck and broke an officer's wrist.

The Equilon submarine vanished from passive tracking.

“Lost?”

“Masked.”

“Or crushed.”

Orren held the hull feed.

There.

The damaged bearing scraped once.

Alive.

The hardliner launched a second weapon toward the port.

Orren had one interceptor.

He could kill the weapon or expose the enemy with a tracking pulse.

The first protected civilians.

The second might end the submarine before it launched again.

“Intercept the port shot.”

“We lose attack.”

“Yes.”

The interceptor left their tube.

Its engine pulled the leviathan's attention across them.

The creature changed direction again.

Every pressure gauge on the command deck moved at once.

The hardliner boat fired active sonar in panic.

For one bright acoustic instant, the sea became visible.

Enemy submarine.

Equilon pursuit boat.

Atlovaian boat.

Two discarded torpedo bodies.

One animal larger than the tactical volume.

The leviathan turned toward the sonar source.

Orren ordered both pursuit boats silent and released a decoy carrying the hardliner's signature.

The creature struck the decoy, then followed the real launch wake.

The hardliner boat abandoned weapons to survive.

Equilon recovered them.

Atlovaia claimed salvage rights.

Orren told both fleets the civilians above did not care who owned a weapon no longer firing.

The hardliner surfaced six hours later with one ballast chamber crushed and thirty-nine people still alive.

The Equilon captain requested permission to place them under military arrest before rescue.

Orren looked at the sea boiling around the damaged hull.

“Rescue first.”

“They fired on evacuation ports.”

“Then they should live long enough to answer for it.”

Atlovaian divers crossed from one rescue lock. Equilon medics crossed from the other. The hardliner crew emerged without weapons, vomiting pressure medicine into masks.

One was the officer whose launch command had begun the attack.

An Equilon diver held the breathing seal over his face anyway.

No camera reached the Deeps.

The act changed no public narrative.

It kept one defendant alive and one rescuer from becoming the person who decided guilt underwater.

* * *

Kieran sheltered inside a city-scale aircraft carrying families, workshops, gardens, clinics, fighters, and enough fuel to make all of them a target.

Lightning moved across the hull.

Enemy drones hid inside the electrical storm.

Ilyana managed Artemis traffic above.

“I cannot distinguish drone heat from flight-deck equipment.”

“Can you distinguish the city?”

“Not if its weapons stay active.”

The aircraft commander refused to disarm.

Kieran stood in a school compartment where children were strapped beneath acceleration mesh.

“Move weapons to cold status,” he told command.

“We lose defense.”

“You gain identity.”

The commander complied.

Artemis recognized the civilian mass and opened an escort corridor.

The hidden drones emerged to attack.

Orbital defense destroyed them away from the hull.

For once, looking less dangerous became protection.

* * *

The aerial city landed nowhere.

It remained above the storm while crews repaired its damaged skin from interior crawlways. Families slept strapped into public corridors because the outer compartments were closed. The school became a triage ward. Its wall still displayed a lesson on the difference between altitude and height.

Kieran sat beside a student whose restraint bruises had been treated with cooling mesh.

“Did we win?” she asked.

“We crossed the corridor.”

“That was not the lesson.”

“What was?”

“The city survives by moving.”

Outside, the aircraft circled because every available landing field belonged to a faction, held unexploded weapons, or could not bear its weight.

“Then the city survived,” Kieran said.

The student looked toward the ward beds. “All of it?”

Kieran followed her gaze.

“No.”

She nodded as if correcting a calculation.

At the front of the schoolroom, a teacher resumed attendance. Not because class had begun. Because parents elsewhere in the city needed to know which children had arrived alive.

Each name answered changed the sound of the room.

Kieran helped call them.

* * *

At Craterheart, Tomas held a geothermal junction.

Enemy infantry advanced.

Corrupted machines attacked both sides.

The titan-scale organism shifted again beneath bombardment.

Heat moved toward settlements on the surface.

The junction had never been designed as a fortress.

Its walls were pressure stone glazed by decades of mineral vapor. Walkways crossed a vertical chamber whose bottom disappeared into red light. Pipes climbed beside them carrying heat toward surface farms, clinics, and districts that knew Craterheart only as a number on a utility bill.

Tomas's proto-Panoply could survive the chamber.

The people maintaining it could not.

Three technicians remained at the vent controls because remote systems had inverted during the first cyberattack. One wore an enemy field jacket over a heat suit. Another had written two children's names across a visor already clouding from sweat.

“Lift status?” Tomas asked.

“One car,” the chief said. “Sixteen capacity. Thirty-two here if you count prisoners.”

“We count prisoners.”

The enemy infantry breached the upper service door.

Tomas raised his weapon.

The first soldier through did not fire.

Behind her, something moved in the dark with the joint rhythm of a corrupted mining frame. Its cutting arm struck both sides of the doorway.

“Machine loose!”

Tomas fired past her.

The round broke the frame's sensor cluster. It continued from remembered geometry and drove its cutter through the rail where two technicians had been standing.

They were already moving.

The enemy soldier pulled one clear.

For eight seconds, the battle forgot which people belonged opposite one another.

Then command channels returned and told everyone to resume.

Below them, the titan shifted.

The chamber floor moved half a degree. Hot mineral water climbed the wall in a sudden sheet. Surface temperature projections changed from dangerous to lethal.

The lift arrived.

Sixteen spaces.

Nobody entered because every uniform expected the other side to seize it.

Tomas stepped between the doors.

“Technicians first.”

His commander answered at once.

“Hold the junction.”

The enemy officer received a different order and looked equally unhappy.

“Secure the lift.”

The lift waited while two chains of authority prepared to kill thirty-two people over sixteen spaces.

The chief technician reached past Tomas and pressed HOLD.

“This car carries twelve if the brake is hot.”

“It says sixteen.”

“It lies politely.”

Tomas looked at the names written on the other technician's visor.

“Twelve technicians and wounded. Car returns.”

“If power holds,” the enemy officer said.

“Then we make it hold.”

The first car rose.

Both forces defended the cable together while corrupted machines came through the service levels. A FreeStack drone with no surviving allegiance crawled along the wall. Tomas caught it before it reached the lift motor and crushed the cutting head against his armor.

The second car descended eight minutes later.

Surface projections showed settlements entering thermal emergency.

The vent decision could not wait for a third.

Tomas could hold the junction or vent the system and lose it.

“If we vent, they take the route,” command said.

“If we do not, cities cook.”

He opened the vent.

Both armies fled the heat plume.

The strategic position vanished into an environment that refused ownership.

The third lift never returned.

Tomas led the remaining people through a service climb while the junction filled with steam. His armor carried one wounded enemy soldier across the chest and dragged a damaged technician by a rescue line. The enemy officer climbed behind him, using a rifle sling to hold the chief's injured arm.

Above, command marked the junction lost.

On the climb, no one had enough breath to say who had lost it.

Amber received all four theaters at once.

She issued one order:

Protect civilian withdrawal. Preserve external defense. Yield terrain where holding it destroys the systems people require afterward.

Objections accompanied the order.

Thea recorded them.

Unified command functioned before settled law possessed it.

* * *

The order reached each theater differently.

Lysa received it while the evacuation train reversed under artillery fire. Yield terrain. Preserve civilians.

Her ground commander demanded she hold the junction.

“The Sovereign's order—”

“I heard it.”

“Then comply.”

“The train cannot turn if the junction falls.”

Lysa detached two empty freight cars and sent them forward as false mass. Enemy targeting followed. The evacuation train crossed the switch under their shadow.

In the Deeps, Orren received the order as vibration through the hull. The hardliner boat had abandoned weapons but remained full of crew.

An Equilon captain requested permission to destroy it.

“It may rearm.”

“It may surrender.”

“May.”

Orren opened a rescue channel. The hardliner captain answered after the leviathan moved away.

At Craterheart, Tomas heard only fragments:

PRESERVE—CIVILIANS—YIELD—

The geothermal junction was already becoming uninhabitable.

He ordered both sides toward the same lift.

Soldiers fought over boarding order until the titan shifted and reminded them whose route they occupied.

In the aerial city, Kieran helped school staff move children from flight-side compartments. The city banked. Artificial gravity tilted. Food trays, books, and frightened bodies moved toward one wall.

One child held the old Skyview joke:

“Do we file the drones?”

“We survive them first.”

Ilyana opened the Artemis corridor.

The city emerged from storm carrying civilians and armed fighters in cold status. Defense let it pass.

At command, the four human markers remained alive.

Thousands around them did not.

Amber refused the report title PLANETARY VICTORY.

Thea entered:

UNIFIED ACTION. OUTCOME INCOMPLETE.

Harmina appeared as one object only on the wall.

Everywhere else, people survived locally.

Command remained awake after the theaters quieted.

The operations wall dimmed, but it did not stop. Repair estimates replaced weapons tracks. Shelter capacities replaced territorial gains. The same officers who had argued over sacrifice now argued over water pumps, landing priority, and which medical route could tolerate another hour of delay.

Emilia brought Amber a bowl of cold grain.

“Eat.”

“The Deeps report is incomplete.”

“It will remain incomplete while you eat.”

Amber lifted the spoon. Her hand shook enough to strike the rim.

Holt saw and looked away. Thea saw and marked the time in the private health record Amber had ordered for every continuity officeholder, including herself.

“Do not archive the spoon,” Amber said.

“I archive incapacity relevant to command.”

“I am tired.”

“So is incapacity.”

Emilia took the operations stylus from Amber’s other hand.

“Five minutes.”

“GalFed approach probability changed.”

“It will still have changed in five minutes.”

Amber ate three mouthfuls. Around her, the planet continued as voices:

A pump crew requesting ceramic seals.

A submarine reporting an unidentified mass in the water.

A train asking where to place people who had no jurisdiction willing to receive them.

An aerial city requesting a weather corridor.

Craterheart requesting medicine without military classification.

No single voice sounded like Harmina.

Together they sounded like the reason the wall existed.

When Amber resumed, she did not ask first who held the most ground.

She asked which unanswered request would kill someone soonest.

## Chapter 35 - Battle for Artemis

Before acceleration, orbital crews secured cups, tools, photographs, prayer objects, children's toys, and anything else capable of becoming a projectile.

They recorded messages.

Some sent them.

Some saved them for deletion if they returned.

Orren did not record a farewell.

He began one six times.

The first addressed Atlovaia and became an argument.

The second addressed Amber and became a defense of the Assembly strike.

The third addressed Iona and stopped after her name.

He deleted each before the station archive could capture a recovery copy.

His navigator watched from the adjacent restraint couch.

“You are allowed an unfinished sentence.”

“Archives finish them for you.”

“People do that. Archives only preserve the opportunity.”

Orren secured a small metal cup inside the couch net. It had come from an Atlovaian submarine and was useless in freefall unless fitted with a lid. He had not fitted one.

“Why bring that?” the navigator asked.

“It reminds me that one environment’s ordinary object is another environment’s projectile.”

“That sounds like something you prepared for an archive.”

“Delete it.”

The navigator did not.

Across Artemis, families completed pressure drills. One parent wrote identification on a child’s arm because loose tags could tear away. Shipyard workers painted surrender frequencies onto their suit sleeves. Technicians removed ceremonial pins and tied back hair. Nothing dramatic distinguished preparation for history from preparation for maintenance except the number of people who believed they might not return.

Nera Serrin wrote her daughter's name on the inside of Tali's sleeve.

External markings could burn. Wrist tags could tear. The ink beneath the cuff might survive long enough for someone to know which refuge roster to call.

Tali held still through the first arm and pulled away on the second.

“It tickles.”

“Good.”

“Why good?”

Nera closed the marker.

“Because you can feel it.”

Her mother, Asha Serrin, secured kitchen knives inside a drawer net. Her father checked the pressure hood beneath Tali's bench for the third time.

The Serrins had maintained Artemis through two decompressions, one lift-cable failure, nineteen major storms below, and political speeches promising orbital homes mattered as much as ground cities. None of those experiences had taught them how to prepare a child for people deliberately opening the station.

“Refuge Three,” Nera said.

“Refuge Two is closer,” Asha answered.

“Two shares ventilation with the traffic spine.”

“Which you will be in.”

“Which makes it a target.”

Asha tightened the drawer net until the cord cut her fingers.

“You are saying this as if the correct refuge makes you less in the target.”

Nera did not answer.

Tali climbed into the pressure hood and spoke through the unsealed face ring.

“Can I take the sky map?”

The flat drawing of Harmina had begun with a circle too small for orbital homes. Tali had added dots around it, then lines, then a yellow mark where Project Ra caught a training asteroid. The paper had been folded until the creases turned white.

“It may tear,” Nera said.

“So might everything.”

Asha stopped pulling the net.

“Give her the map.”

Nera folded it into Tali's inner pocket.

Her traffic credential sounded.

BATTLE AUTHORITY TRANSFER IN THREE MINUTES.

“I have to go.”

Tali climbed out of the hood and wrapped both arms around Nera's waist.

Orbital children learned early not to launch themselves during embraces. They approached carefully, caught fabric, and held without shifting another person's center.

Nera's parents joined them.

Four people made one shape around the bench while station alarms moved from preparation to imminent action.

When the family separated, Tali's identification had smeared across Nera's wrist.

Nera left it there.

Orren opened one final recording.

“I intended to prevent unity,” he said.

Then the launch alarm sounded.

He left the recording open.

On the ground, Kieran projected trajectories. Points of light became probable debris corridors over cities.

Amber issued limits.

No habitat strikes.

No indiscriminate debris.

No Spears fire against occupied platforms.

Life-support surrender accepted regardless of command status.

Tactical officers protested.

“Each limit reduces victory probability.”

“Each defines what victory is,” Amber said.

Emilia attached the limits to the order under Amber's own name.

No subordinate could later claim uncertainty.

* * *

The battle converged from every orbit.

Crewed spacecraft.

Autonomous defense.

Kessler swarms.

Boarding craft.

Shipyard tugs.

Long-horizon weapons.

Artemis used infrastructure as arms.

Docking locks trapped attackers.

Shipyard manipulators threw disabled craft away from habitats.

Lift counterweights altered local trajectories.

Relay beams blinded sensors.

Debris-capture fields became shields.

Nera received the battle as traffic.

The board did not label courage, allegiance, or fear. It showed mass, velocity, pressure status, maneuver authority, docking rights, and whether a craft still answered.

The first attacker crossed a habitat lane disguised beneath a medical priority code.

The code was valid.

The ship carrying it was not medical.

“Challenge.”

No answer.

“Revoke corridor.”

The system refused. Medical priority required two witnesses to cancel.

The second witness station had already lost communication.

Nera opened local audio and heard only pressure alarms from the craft.

It might be an attacker.

It might be a captured ambulance.

Weapons control requested classification.

“Unknown.”

“Unknown is inside the habitat lane.”

“Yes.”

“Then classify hostile.”

Nera looked at the life signs. Eleven. One moving between compartments. Ten restrained or unconscious.

She routed a shipyard tug across the lane.

The tug carried no battle armor. Its manipulators had been built to move reactor shells and station ribs. The pilot was Asha Serrin's younger brother, who had spent breakfast arguing the tug needed new seat padding.

“You want me to catch it?” he asked.

“Move it out of line.”

“It is accelerating.”

“So are you.”

“Traffic people become philosophical when other people hold the controls.”

The tug burned hard.

Its starboard arm caught the unknown craft behind the drive ring. Metal folded. The tug spun with it, both masses crossing within forty meters of the habitat hull.

Weapons fire followed from the attacking formation.

The habitat's debris field caught two rounds.

The third struck the tug's outer arm.

Nera's uncle lost control.

“Release it.”

“Arm is jammed.”

“Cut the joint.”

“Then the craft enters the habitat.”

He used the remaining manipulator to push both ships outward while maintenance charges severed the damaged arm.

The unknown craft tumbled free.

Its medical code stopped.

Hardliner control appeared beneath it.

Weapons destroyed the drive section away from the habitat.

The tug survived with one arm and no attitude thrusters.

“Rescue priority,” Nera said.

“Weapons launch occupies the window.”

“Move it.”

The tactical controller looked at her through the display.

“We lose a clean shot.”

“My uncle loses air.”

“You are not the only family on this board.”

The sentence struck because it was true.

Nera forced herself to look beyond the tug.

A boarding craft carrying twenty-eight people needed the same corridor. A hospital descent waited below. Three station pods drifted after emergency release. The firing solution might stop another attacker from launching against all of them.

She ordered the tug crew into suits and assigned rescue after the weapons launch.

Her uncle acknowledged without accusation.

Nera watched his pressure fall while the firing window counted.

At zero, Artemis fired.

The target broke.

Debris entered two lanes.

The rescue craft launched late and reached the tug with thirty-one seconds of air remaining.

No report called the delay personal.

Nera felt every second in the smeared name on her wrist.

Across another sector, boarding teams approached a hardliner communications ring. The ring rotated too slowly for its design because maintenance crews had shifted mass into refuge compartments. Military plans based on standard rotation missed the dock by twelve meters.

The first boarding craft struck the outer frame and lost its seal.

The second pilot aborted.

Ilyana opened a channel to the refuge.

“Who moved the mass?”

A station cook answered.

“Everyone.”

“Can you restore balance?”

“Not without moving children into the corridor.”

“Do not.”

The boarding team changed approach around the people rather than making people correct the station for war.

Lysa coordinated launch and casualty-return windows from the surface.

She treated orbit like a route with no shoulder.

“Medical descent in ninety seconds.”

“Weapons launch conflicts.”

“Then weapons wait.”

“We lose a firing solution.”

“The wounded lose blood.”

The weapons waited.

* * *

Ilyana won Artemis room by room through permissions.

Hardliners had seized command.

Station technicians retained life support.

She restored local Seal Authority and allowed compartments to reject military overrides.

Families opened doors.

Boarding teams moved through inhabited sections without depressurizing them.

The hardliner commander activated a Kessler cascade aimed at orbital homes.

Orren saw it first.

His craft held the only intercept position.

He transmitted the targeting proof and the final analysis of the Assembly Strike.

“I intended to prevent unity,” he told Amber. “The strike made you necessary.”

“Orren, withdraw.”

“No route.”

“There is always another act.”

“Not another position.”

The cascade deployment opened ahead of him.

Hundreds of compact bodies left the hardliner platform in a disciplined fan. Each carried little mass and enough speed to turn orbital homes into the source of their own destruction. Strike the swarm too broadly and fragments would continue. Strike too late and every habitat would spend days deciding which point of light was a weapon.

Orren's navigator overlaid escape paths.

All crossed the deployment cone.

“Command shell can separate,” she said.

“Crew?”

“Four seats.”

The craft carried nineteen.

“No.”

“The shell can carry targeting proof.”

“So can you.”

She turned toward him.

“That sounded like an order to survive.”

“It was.”

“You do not hold my national command.”

“No.”

“You do hold local flight authority.”

“Until contact.”

She unsealed her couch and moved toward the command shell release.

Around them, crew completed the calculations no one named sacrifice. One technician rerouted coolant from habitation to the forward shield. Another copied engine logs into the public archive. The medic filled nineteen emergency injectors, then put fifteen back.

The navigator stopped at the shell hatch.

“Say the sentence again.”

“Which?”

“The one you left unfinished.”

Orren looked at the open recording indicator.

I intended to prevent unity.

The Assembly chamber returned not as a political conclusion but as fragments: Iona's marker entering the strike center, the bright subject moving two meters, success probabilities updating while human movement became error.

“The strike made Amber necessary,” he said.

“That is analysis.”

“It is true.”

“Not the same.”

The cascade widened.

Targeting predicted impact with the first orbital home in three minutes.

Orren placed the unfinished recording in the shell's archive.

“I killed people to prevent a future I feared. The people I killed do not owe me meaning because I finally understand the result.”

The navigator waited.

“Is that finished?”

“No.”

“Good.”

She entered the shell.

Three injured crew went with her.

The release mechanism jammed halfway.

Orren left the command couch, crossed the deck under acceleration, and struck the manual handle with the metal cup.

The cup bent.

The shell separated.

For one moment, its small lights remained beside them.

Then the craft's acceleration left it behind.

He accelerated into the cascade deployment.

His craft destroyed the control core at contact range.

Debris spread.

The forward shield became white.

One crew member shouted a pressure reading. Another kept reading after the number lost practical meaning. The cascade control core filled the window—not a ship, only a machine whose destruction required everyone aboard Orren's craft to become part of its stopping distance.

Orren did not think of redemption.

He thought of a dissatisfactory hand bell ringing through an Assembly whose public address had failed.

Then contact removed the difference between craft and weapon.

The cascade failed.

Orren's signal ended.

He died protecting the government he had tried to erase.

Iona remained dead.

One act did not purchase the other.

Amber listened to Orren's final transmission twice.

The first time as commander.

The second as the woman he had tried to kill.

“Do we honor him?” Thea asked.

“Record what he did.”

“The public will ask what it means.”

“It means he saved orbital homes.”

“And the Assembly?”

“He destroyed it.”

“People will want one sentence.”

Amber thought of Iona at the dais and Orren entering the cascade.

“Give them two.”

Thea wrote the actions separately.

Orren would not become a redeemed hero.

He would remain a person capable of atrocity, recognition, and sacrifice.

History would have to carry the weight without simplifying him.

The navigator survived in a separated command shell.

Recovery crews found her four hours later with a fractured arm, no external communication, and Orren’s unfinished recording still buffered in the local system. The metal cup had escaped its net and dented the panel above her head.

At the clinic, Ilyana asked whether the recording should be sealed.

“Play it exactly once,” the navigator said.

Amber listened from the answer chamber.

Orren’s voice said, “I intended to prevent unity.”

The launch alarm interrupted him.

No final explanation followed. No request for forgiveness. No statement that transformed his death into a moral conclusion.

“That is all?” Thea asked.

The navigator cradled her repaired arm. “That is what happened next.”

Amber authorized preservation without completion.

The cup entered the same archive case.

Its dent remained.

* * *

Artemis surrendered.

The coalition controlled skywatch, shipyards, the lift, and the external-defense grid.

Amber issued the first unified planetary-defense order.

Thea attached the first recorded dissent.

Kieran added Orren's last transmission to the map layer where his craft disappeared.

Victory celebrations began in some districts.

In orbit, crews repaired air.

Ilyana sat before the answer authority everyone had feared.

The system asked for a planetary commander.

She entered Amber's emergency office and the council witness key together.

No one would answer the sky alone.

* * *

The recorded dissent came from a station technician.

“Unified command saved Artemis,” she said. “It also entered our life-support law without station ratification.”

Ground delegates wanted the dissent delayed until victory observances ended.

Amber ordered it released with the defense order.

“It weakens legitimacy,” Holt said.

“It defines legitimacy.”

Orbital families held no parade.

They repaired hulls.

Oxygen remained below normal. Water carried a metal taste from emergency tanks. Children searched debris lists for personal objects lost during decompression.

Kieran found the flat drawing of Harmina caught in a ventilation grille.

One corner had burned.

He returned it to the child.

“Still wrong?” the child asked.

“Still depends where you stand.”

The child added dots around the circle for orbital homes.

Orren's memorial divided the stations.

Some called him defender.

Others called him the Assembly murderer.

Amber authorized no official title.

His last transmission and strike order were displayed side by side.

Emilia stood before both.

“People will choose one.”

“Then the archive must keep the other,” Amber said.

“Do you forgive him?”

Amber looked at Iona's pin.

“I am not the only person he harmed.”

“Do you thank him?”

“Yes.”

The two answers coexisted without canceling.

The Hall of Service received Orren's name before the political offices agreed whether it belonged there.

Thea entered two sentences:

ORREN THAL-SAITH ORDERED AND EXECUTED THE ASSEMBLY STRIKE.

ORREN THAL-SAITH DIED PREVENTING THE DESTRUCTION OF ORBITAL HOMES.

Neither sentence footnoted the other.

A petition demanded removal.

Another demanded the word *murderer* be replaced by *defender*.

Emilia denied both.

“The Hall is not forgiveness,” she told the families assembled around the Artemis mirror. “It is the refusal to let service or crime erase the person capable of the other.”

Iona Kestrel's entry remained separate.

Her green school pin appeared beside her last speech.

Orren's dented metal cup appeared beside his unfinished sentence.

The Hall placed no line between them.

It placed no equivalence either.

People had to walk the distance themselves.

At Artemis, Ilyana entered the first common defense order with its dissent attached.

The system warned that contradictory authority reduced efficiency.

She accepted the loss.

For three days after the battle, Artemis smelled of hot wiring, sealant, and human waste.

The official casualty count changed each hour as sealed compartments reopened. Some people listed as dead answered from emergency refuges. Others disappeared from the living list after damaged suits finished transmitting. Station families learned not to celebrate a correction until a person crossed a door in front of them.

Ilyana joined a repair crew at the nursery.

The hairline crack had widened during the battle. A patch held, but the wall behind it needed replacement. She supported the panel while the maintenance worker removed its fasteners.

“You command planetary defense now,” the worker said.

“With witnesses.”

“And you are holding a wall.”

“Also with witnesses.”

The worker snorted.

The children had been moved to the adjoining compartment. They passed tools through the door in order of size. Each tool carried a strip of colored fabric so it could be found if released in freefall.

The child who had told Kieran to put Artemis back held out a torque driver.

“Is it back?”

Ilyana looked through the open frame at wiring, insulation, and the black edge of space behind temporary shielding.

“Not yet.”

“When?”

“When you can sleep here without a pressure hood.”

The child considered the answer and passed her the next tool.

No parade marked the moment the wall became airtight.

The ventilation fan changed pitch.

Everyone in the room heard it.

## Chapter 36 - Last Enclave

The final enclave contained too many kinds of enemy to destroy.

Hardliners.

Civilians.

Children.

Archives.

Enhanced soldiers.

Corrupted synthetics.

Medical systems connected to the rest of Harmina.

Malrec walked its shelter levels while screens promised victory.

Families cooked emergency grain beneath them.

Malrec accepted a bowl from a parent who had muted his speech.

The grain was undercooked.

“The heat ration fell,” the parent said.

“Military need.”

“The military is eating?”

“Yes.”

“Then the need is choosing.”

Malrec looked around the shelter.

His doctrine had always treated the people as the reality endangered by Amber's false unity.

Now his command used their food, air, bodies, and children to preserve refusal on terms they had not chosen.

“Would you surrender?” he asked.

“I would feed my child.”

“The new state may erase our law.”

“Your state is erasing dinner.”

The child woke and asked for more.

The parent divided their own portion.

Malrec could not make doctrine answer the bowl.

One parent muted the speech.

Malrec stopped.

“Why?”

“The child is sleeping.”

“The address concerns survival.”

“So does sleep.”

He looked at the child.

The enclave had become dependent on people who did not share his doctrine and could not leave without crossing enemy fire.

Calls for annihilation came from cities the enclave had bombed.

Amber refused.

“We are building the state that will judge them.”

“If they survive,” Irena said, “they continue the war.”

“If we kill the children to prevent that, the war continues through us.”

* * *

Thea and Yara drafted surrender terms.

Medical access regardless of allegiance.

Synthetic quarantine under witness, not destruction.

Enhanced-person treatment protected.

Archives preserved.

Commanders subject to prosecution.

Ordinary refusal protected.

No required declaration that the new state was legitimate.

Sera insisted on the last.

“A surrendered weapon is not a surrendered conscience.”

The terms entered the enclave through maintenance channels because Malrec's command staff blocked the public transmission.

Vent technicians printed copies beside heat alarms.

Clinic workers read them over treatment beds.

Families wrote the medical protections by hand when printers ran out of film.

One copy reached the shelter where Malrec had eaten undercooked grain. The parent read the document aloud while the child slept.

“Medical access regardless of allegiance.”

Someone asked whether medication already inside the enclave would be seized as military supply.

The text did not say.

“Synthetic quarantine under witness.”

A maintenance synthetic asked whether witness meant a person, a camera, or the government performing the quarantine.

The text did not say.

“Ordinary refusal protected.”

The parent stopped.

“What counts as ordinary?”

No one in the shelter knew.

They wrote questions in the margins and sent the page back through the heatline.

Thea received forty-seven copies in one hour.

Each carried different handwriting.

MEDICINE REMAINS WITH PATIENT?

WHO WITNESSES THE WITNESS?

MAY CHILDREN KEEP SCHOOL RECORDS?

DOES REFUSAL INCLUDE MALREC?

IF WE DO NOT DECLARE YOUR STATE LEGITIMATE, MAY WE STILL LEAVE?

Thea and Yara revised the terms without changing the central bargain.
